This humble effort to glorify the divine dikṣā-mantras is lovingly dedicated to my exalted spiritual master, His Divine Grace Śrila A. C. Bhaktivedānta Swami Prabhupāda.

Out of his unlimited mercy he has bestowed the greatest jewels upon this unqualified beggar.

For this, I am eternally indebted to him.

etad eva hi sac-chiṣyaiḥ kartavyaṁ guru-niṣkṛtam yad vai viśuddha-bhāvena sarvārthātmārpaṇaṁ gurau

​​​​​​​“This indeed is the duty of a sincere disciple: to repay the debt to his spiritual master by offering him, with pure heart, his wealth and even his very life.” Sāndipani Muni to Śri Kṛṣṇa and Sudāmā Vipra, in Śrimad-Bhāgavatam 10.80.41

# The Gayatri Book (3ed)

## Maṅgalācaraṇa

Before beginning, I pray that the Lord will allow me to purify my own understanding by writing these pages in the service of the Vaiṣṇavas. Actually, as I enter deeper into the subject, I feel like a blind man undergoing treatment who is slowly beginning to see light and form. I am totally dependent on the guidance of my spiritual master and the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who are kindly leading me along the difficult path from the darkness of material life to the bright world of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

*oṁ ajñāna-timirāndhasya cakṣur unmilitaṁ yena*  
*Śri-caitanya-mano-’bhiṣṭaṁ svayaṁ rūpaḥ kadā mahyaṁ*  
*jñānāñjana-śalākayā tasmai Śri-gurave namaḥ*  
*sthāpitaṁ yena bhū-tale dadāti sva-padāntikam*

**I was born in the darkest ignorance, and my spiritual master opened my eyes with the torch of knowledge. I offer my respectful obeisances unto him. When will Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi Prabhupāda, who has established within this material world the mission to fulfill the desire of Lord Caitanya, give me shelter under his lotus feet?**

*gauraḥ sac-caritāmṛtāmṛta-nidhiḥ gauraṁ sadaiva stute gaureṇa prathitaṁ rahasya bhajanaṁ gaurāya sarvaṁ dade gaurād asti kṛpālur atra na paro gaurasya bhṛtyo ’bhavaṁ*  
*gaure gauravam ācarāmi bhagavān gaura prabho rakṣa mām*

**I pray to Śriman Gaurāṅga Mahāprabhu whose ecstatic transcendental pastimes are like a river of nectar. Gaura has given the path of confidential devotional service. I will completely surrender to Gaura. Is there anyone more merciful than Gaura? I will become a servant of Gaura. I shall preach the glory of Gaura. May my Lord Gaura protect me.**

*ajānu-lambita-bhujau kanakāvadātau*  
*saṅkirtanaika-pitarau kamalāyatākṣau*  
*viśvambharau dvija-varau yuga-dharma-pālau*  
*vande jagat-priya-karau karuṇāvatārau*

**I worship the incarnations of mercy, Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Śri Nityānanda Prabhu, whose arms extend to Their knees, whose complexions are a resplendent yellow, like molten gold, who inaugurated the** ***saṅkirtana*** **movement, whose eyes are like large lotus petals, who nourish the entire universe, who appeared in the families of exalted** ***brāhmaṇas*****, who protect the** ***yuga-dharma*****, and who bestow the highest auspiciousness upon the residents of the material world.**

*jayatāṁ suratau paṅgor mama manda-mater gati*  
*mat-sarvasva-padāmbhojau rādhā-madana-mohanau*

**Glory to the all-merciful Rādhā and Madana-mohana! I am lame and ill advised, yet They are my directors, and Their lotus feet are everything to me.**

*he kṛṣṇa karuṇā-sindho  dina-bandho jagat-pate*  
*gopeśa gopika-kānta rādhā-kānta namo ’stu te*

**O my dear Kṛṣṇa, ocean of mercy, You are the friend of the distressed and the source of creation. You are the master of the cowherd men and the lover of the** ***gopis*****. I offer my respectful obeisances unto You.**

*tapta-kāñcana-gaurāṅgi rādhe vṛndāvaneśvari*  
*vṛṣabhānu-sute devi praṇamāmi hari-priye*

**I offer my respects to Rādhārāṇi, whose bodily complexion is like molten gold and who is the Queen of Vṛndāvana. You are the daughter of King Vṛṣabhānu, and You are very dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa.**

*śri-kṛṣṇa-caitanya prabhu nityānanda*  
*śri-advaita gadādhara śrivāsādi-gaura-bhakta-vṛnda*  
*hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare*  
*hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare*

The subject of this book is the five *gāyatri-mantras* and the two *mūla-mantras* as they are given at second initiation (*dikṣā*) by the *gurus* in disciplic succession from Śrila Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Thākura. These mantras are explained in detail and their general significance covered. I have also presented word meanings, how to chant them properly, the qualifications of chanters, and so on, and illustrated everything with stories from the Vedic tradition.

Special emphasis is placed on meditation methods. Following the formula, “realization according to meditation,” the reader may find useful tips for how to enter more deeply into the understanding and practice of these mantras.

I originally designed this book for my own second-initiated disciples to give them proper guidelines for their spiritual practice. However, while writing I realized that there may be a wider audience of inquisitive readers. Thus I humbly offer this book to all devotees interested in the *gāyatri-mantras*, be they initiated practitioners, candidates aspiring for *mantra-dikṣā*, or mere nectar seekers.

Those who will receive the greatest benefit from reading this book, however, will be those who are fortunate enough to have undergone the authorized and powerful process of *mantra-dikṣā*, formal initiation into the sacred *gāyatri-mantras*.

It is my sincere desire that by studying this publication the readers may increase their enthusiasm and taste for *gāyatri* meditation and thus derive at least a small drop of nectar from the immeasurable ocean of spiritual benefits these mantras afford.

## Foreword by Kṛṣṇa-kṣetra Swami

In 1968, Śrila Prabhupāda, the founder-*ācārya* of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, awarded *brāhmaṇa* initiation to a few of his Western disciples at their recently opened Boston Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa temple. Over the remaining nine years of his life, Prabhupāda would give the mantras of brāhmaṇa initiation – including the *brahma-gāyatri* and other mantras he had received from his guru, Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Ṭhākura – to many more disciples worldwide. These spiritual aspirants had shown themselves committed to the regimen of daily devotional practice and avoidance of the unfavorable habits Prabhupāda had defined.

To give *brāhmaṇa* initiation was a big step, one not without risk to Prabhupāda’s reputation among his godbrothers and other Vaiṣṇavas in India. Willing to take that risk, Prabhupāda saw it as the logical and necessary move toward making “Kṛṣṇa consciousness” a reality beyond the shores of India, among people who had not been born into *brāhmaṇa* families. Brāhmaṇa initiated devotees could begin to learn and practice the process of *arcana*, formal worship of Kṛṣṇa in his *mūrti* (sacred image consecrated according to standard *pañcarātrika* procedures), as in thousands of Indian temples. With *brāhmaṇa* initiation, devotees could pursue the finer aspects of *kṛṣṇa-bhakti* practice and thus deepen their devotion. They would also be able to become increasingly qualified as priests and teachers in Kṛṣṇa’s temples for the growing congregation of Vaiṣṇavas and Vaiṣṇavis worldwide.

As Prabhupāda’s initiated disciples cross the threshold of middle age into old age, and as new generations of Vaiṣṇavas come forth, all feel the need to better comprehend his legacy. If Vaiṣṇava culture is to take root in the West and carry on indefinitely, surely individual practitioners need to have a thorough understanding of their adopted tradition in all its nuanced details so that they can imbibe it in their personal lives and devotional practice.

This book joins an expanding literature of recent years that aims to fill this need. In particular, what Śrila Prabhupāda often called “brahminical culture” – a way of life centered on the “simple living and high thinking” taught in Vedic teachings – will surely be nurtured by H. H. Śacinandana Swami’s present work. The Gāyatri Book provides a wealth of knowledge culled from a range of authoritative Vaiṣṇava sources, much as the Hari-bhakti-vilāsa of Gopāla Bhaṭṭa and Sanātana Gosvāmis, itself a prominent resource for this work, draws on numerous authoritative sources. As a compendium of Vaiṣṇava understanding on the mantras here treated, it serves to “unpack” both the meanings of the mantras and the means for realizing those meanings.

Yet the book is more than a loose compendium. Those who know Śacinandana Swami personally will recognize the imprint of his expertise and concern as a teacher. The Gāyatri Book is both a practical manual and a carefully woven explication, thoughtfully structured as an unfolding, blossoming flower, revealing one after another the layers of significance to this important aspect of *brāhmaṇa* practice.

Surely all devotees who read this book, whether already initiated into the chanting of the *gāyatri-mantras* or aspiring to become so, will win understanding that will facilitate their (our) becoming better practitioners of devotional service. It can then be hoped (as I am hoping) that more devotees of Lord Kṛṣṇa will eagerly take up, along with the activities of personal practice and mission propagation, the practices of temple worship and the teaching of *brāhmaṇa* culture.

Kṛṣṇa-kṣetra Swami.

## Preface. Why this book?

Why this book? A simple answer might be because it is needed. A more elaborate answer points at my own fascination and struggle with the subject. Let me explain.

Throughout the years of my spiritual practice, I have felt a deep yearning gradually arising in my heart to understand and apply the teachings of our glorious *sampradāya*. Once, sitting next to a godbrother while both of us were chanting our *dikṣā-mantras*, I noticed that he needed much more time to finish his *gāyatri* than I did. He also seemed to relish his practice to a degree I had neither seen nor myself felt. Being determined to discover his secret, I took him aside after he had finally come to the end of his mantras, and he mercifully told me many details about the *gāyatri-mantras*. This information had simply been unknown to me before this conversation. But this incident awoke my eagerness to research this field.

For a long time I collected quotes and various other material about *gāyatri*, and gradually the world of the divine mantras began to reveal a few of its secrets to me. I also noticed, however, that the more I learned, the more obliged I felt to seriously apply the knowledge I had gained.

I must admit that I was forced to learn to concentrate on my mantras the hard way, by the path of mistake and correction. A time came in my life when my intense traveling schedule left me negligent toward chanting gāyatri at the proper times and places or with the proper pronunciation and meditation. Finally, one evening when I sat to “meditate” on the mantras, my mind overcome with distraction, I realized sadly that the mantras had left me. Although I had always dutifully chanted them, I had done so without affection or taste and they were simply gone.

Yes, they were gone. In their place I was left with the empty shells of the mantras. I felt like a hungry man holding empty nutshells while watching others enjoy fresh nuts.

Reform was inevitable. I studied my notes, prayed intensely, collected more material, practiced more sincerely, and finally, the mantras returned, hesitatingly at first, like a person who has been hurt who watches from a distance before daring an approach. Eventually, they came closer.

I am still a bad chanter; much in my practice can be improved. But at least I am confident that these wonderful mantras contain everything, and that they will reveal more and more in due course of time.

With great humility I pray that my readers will find important jewels in this book. I also pray that those who have received the mantras can be saved from the same difficult experience I had to suffer.

Some practical advice: This book is filled with information. You may find it difficult, therefore, to read the book straight through. I would suggest that after you have read one or two chapters, you revisit those parts of the book that attract your attention. To assist you in this, the appendix of this 3rd edition contains an additional list of questions for each chapter that will help you identify some of the main points of the chapter. I also suggest that you don’t try to apply everything at once. Rather, look for what is practical and helpful for your present stage and work toward applying that. Better yet, ask someone who knows you and whom you trust to advise you for help in applying the principles taught in this book.

Last but not least, I wish to say that it would be audacious of me to claim authorship of this book. At best I am a faithful collector of divine information. May the kind readers derive pleasure and inspiration from this work and bless me from their hearts.

Oṁ tat sat  
Śacinandana Swami  
September, 2014

## Introduction. The need for a guide

Imagine you are on pilgrimage. You enter a remote forest and discover a large and beautiful temple. It is old, but well preserved. It seems uninhabited. No one appears to be worshiping here anymore, and you feel free to enter the building and walk around at your ease. Soon you find yourself absorbed by the complexity of halls, doorways, attractively furnished rooms, flights of stairs, beautifully arranged garden groves, and other amazing and sometimes bewildering details.

After some time you feel that your initial curiosity has been satisfied. Slightly exhausted you sit down to rest. As you look around, you see more and more detail. Your mind again becomes captivated by the urge to explore. So many new and unknown things are waiting to be discovered! Excited, you jump to your feet and continue your investigation.

But somehow you feel insecure, bewildered, confronted as you are by an overwhelming variety of impressions. You begin to feel the need for an expert guide, someone well-versed in the history of this ancient place who can introduce you to the stories of the former Deities and their worshipers, someone who can explain the architecture, artwork, and meaning and use of the various rooms. Only then will you feel you know something about this place.

A similar experience may occur on pilgrimage to the sacred realm of the *gāyatri-mantras*. What in the beginning, after having received your *mantra-dikṣā*, appears to be just another aspect of your *sādhana*, develops into a complex subject as soon as you sincerely dive into a deeper understanding of *gāyatri*. The scriptures provide so many explanations, and thus there are many aspects of it to consider – and so many fine details to discover – that you can only make sense of it by accepting the help of an expert guide.

To illustrate this point, let us look at the sacred syllable *oṁ*, the beginning of the *brahma-gāyatri-mantra*. From Bhagavad-gitā we learn that *oṁ* is non-different from Brahman and that by chanting it one can attain the spiritual world. And as Kṛṣṇa later explains, *oṁ* has to be chanted at the beginning of each performance of sacrifice, charity, and penance in order to attain the Supreme. Yet on the highest level, *oṁ* – as a combination of the letters *a*, *u*, and *m* – represents Kṛṣṇa, Rādhārāṇi, and the *jiva* soul. But that is not all. We hear that the sacred syllable *oṁ* includes all the Vedas. Moreover, the *Upaniṣads* explain that the whole creation has emanated from *oṁ*.

How can we understand all this without help? All we have seen is a sign that looks like ॐ. A knower of the Sanskrit language will recognize this sign as a combination ofअ (the letter a),ु (the letter u), and ँ (the letter ṁ). But knowing this, can we gain deeper realization simply by reading the sign or hearing or chanting oṁ? Most of us might perceive a feeling of spiritual elevation by doing so, but only rare souls will recognize all the implicit secrets without help.

Hence just by the first syllable of the *brahma-gāyatri-mantra* – *oṁ* – we get an idea of how much is waiting to be discovered in these mantras. Just as a banyan seed contains a huge tree that can bring forth many more seeds, these mantras contain a wealth of information that can bring forth deep realizations from the chanter.

In order to understand the *gāyatri-mantras* we would do well to accept the guidance of Śrila Prabhupāda and our *ācāryas*, carefully following in their footsteps. Then we will not get lost.

This book offers guidance along the path. Of course, it cannot replace our bona fide spiritual master, whom we may approach with further questions and for specific guidance.

With such guidance we will surely be able to enter the wonderful temple of the *gāyatri-mantras*, understand its various rooms, and derive great benefit from our enhanced understanding. We will be able to enter this temple three times daily and come out mentally and spiritually nourished, enlightened in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

## I. What is Gayatri?

## 1. Some definitions of gāyatri

The ways to define *gāyatri* are as manifold as the angles from which one can look at these *mantras*. The following explanations are helpful for developing an understanding according to our tradition.

***Gāyatri*** **delivers one from material existence**

The word *gāyatri* is derived from *gāya* – singing, and *tra* – protecting or delivering. Based on this etymological meaning, Śrila Prabhupāda quoted the following verse to explain the meaning of *gāyatri*:

*gāyantaṁ trāyate yasmād gāyatri tvaṁ tataḥ smṛtā*

**One who chants the** ***gāyatri-mantras*** **is gradually delivered from the material clutches. In other words, that which delivers us from material entanglement is called** ***gāyatri*****.**

In other words, since the mantra, being chanted (*gāyantam*), protects one (*trāyate*), it is called *gāyatri.*

***Gāyatri*** **is Kṛṣṇa Himself**

In the Bhagavad-gitā, Kṛṣṇa identifies the *gāyatri-mantra* as a sound vibration identical with Himself: *gāyatri chandasām aham* – “Of poetry I am the *gāyatri*.” Śrila Prabhupāda states in his purport to this verse:

**Because the** ***gāyatri-mantra*** **is especially meant for God realization, it represents the Supreme Lord. This mantra is meant for spiritually advanced people, and when one attains success in chanting it, he can enter into the transcendental position of the Lord. \[…\] The** ***gāyatri-mantra*** **is very important in Vedic civilization and is considered to be the sound incarnation of Brahman. Brahmā is its initiator, and it is passed down from him in disciplic succession.**

***Gāyatri*** **is meant to Worship Kṛṣṇa**

There are several *gāyatri-mantras* given in our *gaudiya-sampradāya* to qualified disciples. All of them worship Śri Kṛṣṇa in His various aspects:

- The *brahma-gāyatri* worships Lord Kṛṣṇa as the one who enthuses the life of His devotees.
- The *guru-gāyatri,* by which we worship the spiritual master, can be seen as indirect worship of Kṛṣṇa. *Sākṣād dharitvena samasta-śāstraiḥ* – all scriptures see the spiritual master as nondifferent from Kṛṣṇa. *Śri guru* is *sevaka-bhagavān*, the Supreme Lord in His form as a servant.
- The *gaura-gāyatri* worships Lord Kṛṣṇa as Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu, who is addressed in this mantra by the name Viśvambhara, “the maintainer of the world.”
- The *gopāla-gāyatri* worships Lord Kṛṣṇa in His three aspects as Madana-mohana, Govinda, and Gopinātha, the presiding Deities of the processes of *sambandha*, *abhidheya*, and *prayojana*.
- The *kāma-gāyatri* worships the Lord as the transcendental Cupid who invites all the conditioned souls into His loving pastimes.
- Another more confidential understanding, which will be revealed later, sees Śrimati Rādhārāṇi being worshiped especially in the *kāma-gāyatri* and the *brahma-gāyatri*.

***Gāyatri*** **is themother of the Vedas**

The Brahma-saṁhitā describes *gāyatri* as *trayi-mūrti-mayi,* the “mother of the Vedas.” Later we will hear how *gāyatri* has been manifest from the transcendental sound vibration of Kṛṣṇa’s flute and how Lord Brahmā has received complete Vedic knowledge from these mantras. Just as the holy name is nondifferent from Kṛṣṇa, His flute song, *gāyatri*, is identical with the Lord. Thus all Vedic knowledge effortlessly emanates by means of the *gāyatri-mantras* from the Lord.

## 2. The effect of chanting Gāyatri

**Liberation and real life**

As cited above, *gāyatri* liberates us from material life: *gāyantaṁ trāyate yasmād gāyatri tvaṁ tataḥ smṛtā*. For the conditioned soul tightly bound by the powerful material energy, there is no greater relief than liberation. The material energy is a foreign environment for the soul, and just as a prisoner feels relieved to be allowed to leave the foreign environment of prison and return to his familiar life, so the soul feels relieved when freed from the external land of matter.

The idea that liberation is the greatest achievement possible, however, is seen only from the negative viewpoint; it is a prisoner’s perspective. Liberation delivers us from material suffering but offers nothing more. It is an impersonal conception because it will ultimately lead to a complete negation of variety, activity, and even individuality. Where, then, is real life?

When we look at liberation from a Vaiṣṇava’s perspective, a positive picture emerges. The Śrimad-Bhāgavatam defines *mukti* as *muktir hitvānyathā-rūpaṁ svarūpeṇa vyavasthitiḥ*, to be situated in one’s constitutional position as an eternal servitor of Kṛṣṇa. For a Vaiṣṇava, liberation is much more than the mere negation of material existence. It means becoming engaged in Kṛṣṇa’s transcendental loving service. It gives real life: real activities and true exchanges between real persons. Such a person will be so positively engaged in his new life that he will instantly forget the dark, desperate prison cell of his former conditioned life. Śrila Prabhupāda describes it in the following words:

**This transcendental devotional service of the Lord is so wonderful that the occupation keeps the deserving devotee always rapt in psychological activities, without deviation from the absolute touch. Thus love of Godhead, developed in the heart of the devotee, is a great mystery.**

This is what is meant when we speak about a “positive life of liberation.” Far from being a mere cessation of material life, such positive liberation means entering into a loving relationship with Kṛṣṇa. Once, in a mood of great urgency, Śrila Prabhupāda said:

**That is real necessity.** ***govinda-viraheṇa me*** **– Govinda necessity. The necessity is Govinda. And He is not alone. There is again life, there is again Vṛndāvana, again** ***gopis*****, again dancing, again eating, again everything. That is real necessity.**

To summarize, if we define *gāyatri* as that which liberates from material life, we implicitly speak of the Vaiṣṇava concept of liberation, namely entering the unalloyed, purely transcendental loving service for Śri Śri Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa.

**A twofold blessing**

Although gāyatri is meant to liberate the chanter from material influence, it also helps him to fulfill all his desires, even if they are materially tinged. It doesn’t matter how great or extraordinary one’s desires are – there is no limit to the *gāyatri-mantras’* capacity to fulfill them. The Brahma-saṁhitā describes that by the power of the *gopāla-mantra* Lord Brahmā was able to create the material universe.

How do we understand this? Why should a mantra that is supposed to free us from all material influence simultaneously fulfill our material desires? We can find the answer to this question in Lord Brahmā’s story. After Brahmā received the *gopāla-mantra* from Lord Nārāyaṇa’s consort, goddess Sarasvati Devi, she said, “O Brahmā, this mantra will assuredly fulfill your heart’s desire.” Śrila Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Thākura comments:

**The mantra \[…\] has a twofold aspect. One aspect is that it tends to make the pure soul run after all-attractive Śri Kṛṣṇa, the Lord of Gokula and the divine milkmaids. \[…\] When the devotee is free from all sorts of mundane desires and willing to serve the Lord he attains the fruition of his heart’s desire, viz., love of Kṛṣṇa.**

**But in the case of the devotee who is not of unmixed aptitude this superexcellent mantra also fulfills his heart’s desire. The transcendental** ***kāma-bija*** **is inherent in the divine logos located in Goloka; and the** ***kāma-bija*** **pervertedly reflected in the worldly affairs satisfies all sorts of desires of this mundane world.**

In other words, material desires pervertedly reflect our original pure desire to meet and serve the Lord. Therefore, although the *gāyatri-mantras* are meant to fulfill our pure transcendental desires, if a chanter’s consciousness is pervertedly reflected and roams the material world, the *gāyatri-mantras* will also fulfill his or her material desires. While chanting *gāyatri*, please be conscious of your thoughts!

Brahmā is certainly an extraordinary personality, and thus his material desires are also extraordinary. He is Brahmā, lord of creation. He did not desire a house with a garden, dog, and swimming pool like an average middle-aged man of the world’s “civilized” countries. Rather, he felt a deep urging to create a universe. Since he is also a great devotee of the Lord, he wanted to use his creative potential in Kṛṣṇa’s service. Although Brahmā’s desires were immensely great and somewhat materially motivated, still, by the power of the *gāyatri-mantras* they were all completely fulfilled.

But this is only one side of the blessing he received. The other side is that by chanting the mantras and performing his devotional service in the form of universal creation, his material motivations were gradually purified and his love for Kṛṣṇa accordingly increased.

This is how Kṛṣṇa works on conditioned souls through the mantra’s potency. Kṛṣṇa knows that He will not make ordinary persons of this world ultimately happy by fulfilling the desires that separate them from His service. Therefore while fulfilling His devotees’ desires, Kṛṣṇa simultaneously purifies them so that in the end they want only His lotus feet.

When praising human birth on Bharata-varṣa (Earth) for offering the great opportunity for self-realization, the demigods expressed the same idea:

*satyaṁ diśaty arthitam arthito nṛṇāṁ*  
*naivārthado yat punar arthitā yataḥ*  
*svayaṁ vidhatte bhajatām anicchatām*  
*icchāpidhānaṁ nija-pāda-pallavam*

**The Supreme Personality of Godhead fulfills the material desires of a devotee who approaches Him with such motives, but He does not bestow benedictions upon the devotee that will cause him to demand more benedictions again. However, the Lord willingly gives the devotee shelter at His own lotus feet, even though such a person does not aspire for it, and that shelter satisfies all his desires. That is the Supreme Personality’s special mercy.**

Whenever a devotee prays to Kṛṣṇa to fulfill a selfish desire, the Lord undoubtedly does so, but He does not award anything that, after being enjoyed, will cause the devotee to ask for further material blessings. If devotees have desires for enjoyment separate from Kṛṣṇa but still engage in the Lord’s service, Kṛṣṇa forcibly gives them shelter at His lotus feet. There they will forget their other desires. Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu explains Kṛṣṇa’s compassionate nature by paraphrasing His thoughts in the following sweet words:

**If one engages in My transcendental loving service but at the same time wants the opulence of material enjoyment, he is very, very foolish. Indeed, he is just like a person who gives up** ***ambrosia*** **to drink poison. Since I am very intelligent, why should I give this fool material prosperity? Instead I shall induce him to take the nectar of the shelter of My lotus feet and make him forget illusory material enjoyment.**

Kṛṣṇa may indeed fulfill our material desires through our chanting of the *gāyatri-mantras*, but He will do it in such a way that we become purified and ultimately “run after all-attractive Śri Kṛṣṇa,” as Śrila Bhaktisiddhānta Mahārāja states. Lord Brahmā experienced this twofold blessing when he chanted the *gopāla-gāyatri,* and Gopa-kumāra had a similar realization. Both obtained the Lord’s full shelter after their respective desires had been fulfilled by the *gopāla-mantra’s* power.

**A personal experience**

As I was writing these lines, Kṛṣṇa gave me the opportunity to personally experience the potency of the *gāyatri-mantras*’ ability to fulfill material desires. Staying on a remote island in Croatia for my *kārttika-vrata*, I used to take a daily health walk through the beautiful countryside. One day, as I left the path, I stumbled and cut my foot deeply. Knowing that an infection would impede my spiritual life and service, but reluctant to leave the lovely island for medical treatment, I decided to conduct an experiment. When I chanted the *gopāla-gāyatri* I focused on a mental request: “My dear Lord, please arrange that this wound heals quickly and does not become infected.”

After pronouncing this material desire, I continued to chant my *japa*. About an hour later, when I had finished my rounds, I looked at my wound and was surprised. In that short hour, the cut had almost closed and a scab had already begun to form. There was no sign of pus, and the wound stopped being a source of concern.

I had already had a similar experience to this one when I was on a sacred pilgrimage high in the Himalayas. Stumbling suddenly, I found myself lying helplessly on the ground four meters from the path, feeling a terrible but familiar pain. “Oh no, I have at the least torn a ligament but might have broken my foot. Where will I get a plaster cast in the mountains?” The only “clinic” left for me was the *gopāla-mantra*. For one hour I sheepishly chanted the mantra, remembering Śrila Prabhupāda’s high standard of approaching Kṛṣṇa for spiritual, not medical blessings. Yet after having finished the chanting I was “healed” and was able to continue with only a slight limp.

Such “miracles,” however, are only an insignificant by-product of chanting *gāyatri*. Let us not forget the real goal of life, as Śrila Prabhupāda has expressed so nicely:

**The real purpose of life is to go back home, back to Godhead and live with the Supreme Personality of Godhead, play with the Supreme Personality of Godhead, dance with the Supreme Personality of Godhead and eat with the Supreme Personality of Godhead. These are different items of** ***ānanda*****, spiritual happiness in spiritual variegatedness. \[…\] One can fulfill the ultimate goal of life only by engaging in devotional service to the Lord. Then the Lord will give one proper instructions on how to go back home, back to Godhead.**

Going back home, back to Godhead will heal all our wounds – those suffered in the past, those we suffer from in the present, and those we will suffer in the future.

## 3. What does dikṣā mean?

In our tradition we know two kinds of *dikṣā*: After having received *nāma-dikṣā,* initiation into the chanting of the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra, a disciple who has shown his sincerity and eligibility will be given *mantra-dikṣā*, initiation into the *gāyatri-mantras*. Bhaktivinoda Thākura says bestowing the name and initiation with the *gāyatri-mantras* are two separate functions, and although they are generally performed by the same *guru*, sometimes they are performed by different *gurus*. He who first initiates a disciple into the holy name is called the *nāma-guru*, and he who initiates a disciple into the *gāyatri-mantras* is called the *dikṣā-guru*. In this book, we refer to second initiation whenever we are speaking of *dikṣā*.

To understand *gāyatri* it is essential to be aware of the meaning of *dikṣā*. Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi defines *dikṣā* by quoting the following well-known verse:

*divyaṁ jñānaṁ yato dadyāt kuryāt pāpasya saṅkṣayam*  
*tasmāt dikṣeti sā proktā deśikais tattva-kovidaiḥ*

***Dikṣā*** **is the process that awakens one’s transcendental knowledge (*****divya-jñāna*****) and vanquishes all reactions caused by sinful activity. A person expert in the study of the revealed scriptures knows this process as** ***dikṣā*****.**

This verse hints at an important point, namely, the close connection and interdependence between *dikṣā* (formal initiation by giving mantras) and *śikṣā* (the conveyance of transcendental knowledge). To understand clearly why *dikṣā* is necessary and what the “transmittal of transcendental knowledge” means, let us examine Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi’s Bhakti-sandarbha text in detail. Before quoting the above verse, he writes:

*yady api śri-bhāgavata-mate pañcarātrādi-vad arcana-*  
*mārgasyāvaśa katvaṁ nāsti, tad vināpi śaraṇāpatty-ādinām*  
*ekatareṇapi puruṣārtha-siddher abhihitatvāt.*  
*tathāpi śri-nāradādi-vartmānusā radbhiḥ śri-bhagavatā saha*  
*sambandha-viśeṣaṁ dikṣā-vidhānena śri-guru-caraṇa-sampāditaṁ*  
*cikirṣadbhiḥ kṛtāyāṁ dikṣāyām arcanam avaśyaṁ kriyetaiva*

**In the opinion of Śrimad-Bhāgavatam there is no absolute necessity of following the methods of** ***pañcarātra*****, such as** ***arcana*** **\[Deity worship\]. Even without them one can achieve the full perfection of life simply by practicing even one of the nine methods of devotional service, such as** ***śaraṇāpatti*** **(full surrender).**  
**Nonetheless, if those who follow the paths of authorities like Śri Nārada desire to obtain their specific relationship with the Personality of Godhead, they can only achieve it by taking shelter of the feet of their divine spiritual master, which means they accept** ***dikṣā*** **from him and then perform the process of** ***arcana*****.**

Then Jiva Gosvāmi underlines his statement by quoting the following *śloka*:

*ato guruṁ praṇamyaiva sarva-svaṁ vinivedya ca*  
*gṛhṇiyād vaiṣṇavaṁ mantraṁ dikṣa-pūrvaṁ vidhānataḥ*

**Therefore, one should first bow down to his** ***guru*****, offer everything he possesses to him, and accept a** ***vaiṣṇava-mantra*** **by properly carrying out the process of** ***dikṣā*****.**

In other words, although the nine processes of *bhakti*  are sufficient in themselves to bestow the full perfection of life (*puruṣa-artha-siddhi*), one still has to accept a bona fide spiritual master in order to follow the *sampradāya’s* path. This means that one must first accept *dikṣā* and then carry out the activities connected to this process, such as *arcana*.

At this point, by quoting the *divyaṁ jñānam* verse from above, Jiva Gosvāmi defines *dikṣā* as a process by which transcendental knowledge is awakened. He then explains what is meant here by *divya-jñāna*:

*divyaṁ jñānaṁ hy atra śrimati mantre bhagavat-svarūpa-jñānaṁ,*  
*tena bhagavatā sambandha-viśeṣaṁ ca.*

***Divyaṁ jñānam*****, or transcendental knowledge, here means knowledge of the identity of the Personality of Godhead within one’s divine mantra, and also knowledge of one’s specific, individual relationship (*****sambandha-viśeṣa*****) with the Supreme Lord.**

The word *atra*, “here,” indicates that this definition is specifically meant for this discussion on *dikṣā*, even though other definitions of *divya-jñāna* may be appropriate for other applications. Concluding, Jiva Gosvāmi quotes two ślokas that more clearly describe the importance of *dikṣā*:

*dvijānām anupanitānāṁ sva-karmādhyayanādiṣu*  
*yathādhikāro nāstiha syāc copanayād anu*

*thātrādikṣitānāṁ tu  mantra-devārcanādiṣu*  
*nādhikaro ’sty ataḥ kuryād atmānaṁ Śiva-saṁstutam*

***Brāhmaṇas*** **who have not been initiated are not authorized to engage in their prescribed duties of studying the Vedas and so on. But after initiation they are authorized.**  
**Similarly those who have not taken** ***dikṣā*** **are not authorized to perform such activities as chanting mantras and worshiping the Deity of the Lord. Therefore one should make oneself auspicious and reputable \[by accepting initiation\].**

*Dikṣā* is described here as the formal process of receiving a *vaiṣṇava-mantra,* by which one can begin the practices of devotional service according to the *pañcarātrika* system. Such practices include Deity worship and the chanting of *bija*- and *gāyatri-mantras*. These mantras will reveal the Lord’s personal form in due course of time and will also disclose to a devotee his or her specific relationship with the Lord in one of the transcendental *rasas* (*dāsya, sakhya, vātsalya,* or *mādhurya*).

The difference, therefore, between *śikṣā* and *dikṣā* is that the latter bestows transcendental knowledge through a formal process, namely, accepting a mantra in an initiation ceremony. This is a necessary prerequisite for those who wish to take full advantage of *śikṣā*, spiritual instruction.

**The “magic” of** ***dikṣā***

The purpose of *dikṣā* is to bring the disciple to the point of realizing his relationship with Kṛṣṇa. This occurs through the gift of a mantra from the *guru* and the taking of vows in a ceremony. Because this ceremony marks a new beginning, indeed a new spiritual birth, in a devotee’s life, the word *dikṣā* refers to the formal event, the formal commitment.

However, the *dikṣā* ceremony is far more than a formality. It is founded on deep, transcendental reality. The *dikṣā* ceremony formalizes a relationship that existed before the ceremony. The gradual development and deepening of the guru-disciple relationship is a maturing process that would have begun long before the meeting at the *yajña* fire. In some cases, it may even be the continuation of a relationship carried out in past lives. The initiation ceremony formalizes the relationship, substantiates it with a *yajña* in which initiation vows are accepted, and is blessed with an exchange of transcendental gifts.

We can compare the initiation ceremony to a wedding ceremony. Husband and wife do not begin their relationship during the marriage ceremony. Rather, the marriage ceremony recognizes and formalizes a relationship that had begun some time before.

That the initiation ceremony serves to formalize an already existing relationship does not minimize its importance. Every *gṛhastha* who has undergone a *vivāha-yajña* will confirm that the ceremony creates deep *saṁskāras*, impressions that bind the couple together on the subtle plane. The same holds true for the *dikṣā* ceremony. Something substantial takes place during the ritual: the disciple accepts vows and the *guru* imparts the mantra. While the ceremony itself may be a formality, the vows and the transmission of the mantra are definitely not. They form the subtle substance of the initiation.

The extent and profundity of the ceremony are dependent on the disciple’s sincerity to adhere to his or her vows, and on the guru’s transparency in representing the tradition (*sampradāya*) in which the mantras are handed down. If both sincerity and transparency are present, something mystical – or better yet, transcendental – will happen. At the time of *mantra-dikṣā,* the guru will impart both the mantra’s inconceivable potency along with his own experience in chanting it. This process can neither be seen nor intellectually explained. Spiritual life is not purely rational. It is experienced on many levels simultaneously, some easily grasped by the intellect and some not. It is this transcendental reality behind the initiation ceremony that makes *dikṣā* so important to a disciple’s spiritual life.

In understanding how initiation is not merely a formality, we must also remember that Kṛṣṇa actively intervenes in a devotee’s life at the time of *dikṣā*. Caitanya Mahāprabhu confirms:

*dikṣā-kāle bhakta kare ātma-samarpaṇa*  
*sei-kāle kṛṣṇa tāre kare ātma-sama*  
*sei deha kare tāra cid-ānanda-maya*  
*aprākṛta-dehe tāṅra caraṇa bhajaya*

**At the time of initiation, when a devotee fully surrenders unto the service of the Lord, Kṛṣṇa accepts him to be as good as Himself. When the devotee’s body is thus transformed into spiritual existence, the devotee, in that transcendental body, renders service to the lotus feet of the Lord.**

Thus the formal process of *dikṣā* substantiates the relationship not only between disciple and *guru* but between disciple and Kṛṣṇa. Śrila Prabhupāda once expressed this idea in a simple and convincing way: “Second initiation means he is recognized: ‘Now he has become a fully competent Vaiṣṇava.’”

Since *dikṣā* virtually transforms our existence, it is considered a second birth, as Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi confirms:

*yathā kāñcanatāṁ yāti kāṁsyaṁ rasa-vidhānataḥ*  
*tathā dikṣā-vidhānena dvijatvaṁ jāyate nṛṇām*

**As bell metal, when mixed with mercury, is transformed into gold, simply by the initiation process (*****dikṣā-vidhānena*****) a person, even though not golden pure, can be transformed into a** ***brāhmaṇa*** **or** ***dvija*** **(twice-born).**

We should also consider that initiation is not an isolated event taking place only at the time of the *dikṣā* ceremony. Rather, it is an ongoing process and is only complete when we have achieved full surrender. Therefore Śrila Prabhupāda often referred to initiation as the beginning of spiritual life.

## 4. The holy name and the gāyatri-mantras

All Vedic scriptures praise the exalted glories of chanting the holy name above any other method of devotional service. Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu even said that one can complete all nine processes of devotional service simply by chanting the holy name. Why, then, do we need the *gāyatri-mantras*?

We need them because the ultimate result – *kṛṣṇa-prema* – can only be attained if we chant the holy name without offense. As the perfect teacher, the Supreme Lord in His form as Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu instructs us by taking the role of a conditioned soul:

*nāmnām akāri bahudhā nija-sarva-śaktis*  
*tatrārpitā niyamitaḥ smaraṇe na kālaḥ*  
*etādṛśi tava kṛpā bhagavan mamāpi*  
*durdaivam idṛśam ihājani nānurāgaḥ*

**My dear Lord, although You bestow Your mercy upon the fallen conditioned souls by liberally teaching Your holy names, I am so unfortunate that I commit offenses while chanting the holy name, and therefore I do not achieve attachment for chanting.**

Because the ordinary conditioned souls are so completely covered by an almost impenetrable cloud of *anartha*, material desire, and offense, they cannot chant the holy name purely. At best they chant *nāmābhāsa*, by which they obtain wonderful preliminary results, including freedom from sinful reactions. But they do not achieve the final goal of chanting, *prema*. *Prema* cannot be achieved unless we chant with pure consciousness (*śuddha-nāma*). But before being able to chant *śuddha-nāma*, our *anarthas* must be completely eradicated and firm attachment for the Lord’s devotional service must be established in the heart.

To help us do this, the merciful *ācāryas* have given us Deity worship, for which we require the *gāyatri-mantras*. Deity worship is a powerful method of purifying the heart. It is therefore highly recommended that devotees accept second initiation, receive the *gāyatri-mantras*, and engage in *arcana*, following the prescribed rules and regulations in order to worship the Lord properly.

Śrila Prabhupāda explains why accepting *mantra-dikṣā* helps us chant the holy name:

**By chanting the holy name of the Lord, one can reach the platform of love of Godhead. One might ask, then, what is the necessity of being initiated \[by** ***mantra-dikṣā*****\]? The answer is that even though the chanting of the holy name is sufficient to enable one to progress in spiritual life to the standard of love of Godhead, one is nonetheless susceptible to contamination because of possessing a material body. Consequently, special stress is given to the** ***arcana-vidhi*****.**

The activities of *arcana-vidhi*, namely, Deity worship combined with the chanting of *gāyatri-mantras*, elevate the *sādhaka* to *sattva-guṇa* or purity. Further progress is much easier from that pure platform. Deity worship helps an aspiring transcendentalist become free from the disturbances of the lower modes of nature, enabling him to overcome the covering cloud of *māyā* and regain his original spiritual understanding as the Lord’s eternal servant.

Therefore Śrila Prabhupāda requested his disciples initiated into chanting the *gāyatri-mantras* to approach the Deity on the altar and worship Him. This will help them solidify their relationship with the Lord. When their mantra chanting and Deity worship mature, they will actually develop their specific relationship with Kṛṣṇa. This becomes possible because the mantra is invested with *divya-jñāna*, transcendental knowledge of the Lord’s form. It is this potency that awakens one’s specific relationship with the Lord.

To summarize: one important function of *mantra-dikṣā* is to enable the disciple to worship the Lord’s Deity and thus overcome the *anarthas* in his heart. There is, however, also a direct relationship between the *gāyatri-mantras* and the holy name. While speaking to the great Māyāvādi leader

Prakāśānanda Sarasvati, Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu explained the interdependence of mantra and *nāma* according to a concise formula:

*kṛṣṇa-mantra haite habe saṁsāra-mocana*  
*kṛṣṇa-nāma haite pābe kṛṣṇera caraṇa*

**By chanting** ***kṛṣṇa-mantra*** **\[the** ***gopāla-gāyatri*****\] one will be freed from material existence \[*****saṁsāra*****\]. By chanting** ***kṛṣṇa-nāma*** **\[the holy name of Kṛṣṇa or the Hare Kṛṣṇa** ***mahā-mantra*****\] one will attain Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet.**

In other words, *kṛṣṇa-mantra* and *kṛṣṇa-nāma* work closely together to bring the chanter to Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet. Both have different functions in this task. There is, however, a different perspective that does not distinguish between *nāma* and mantra: Although initiation into *harināma* and *mantra-dikṣā* are two discrete functions, they are generally performed by the same *guru.* That is, the *dikṣā-guru* and the *nāma-guru* \[the spiritual master who initiates the disciple into chanting *harināma*\] are usually identical. Bhaktivinoda Thākura states:

**The** ***nāma-guru*** **teaches the spiritual conclusions and reveals the confidential nature of the holy name. Generally, the** ***dikṣā-guru*** **will also be the** ***nāma-guru*** **– and the** ***dikṣā-mantra*** **is actually the holy name.**

**The mantra loses its meaning and purpose if it is separated from the holy name; simply by uttering the holy name of the Lord, the mantra is automatically chanted.**

Yet from the absolute perspective, nāma is always seen as superior to mantra. Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi explains why:

*nanu bhagavan-nāmātmakā eva mantraḥ \| tatra viśeṣeṇa namaḥ-*  
*śabdādy-alaṁkṛtaḥ śri-bhagavatā śrimad-ṛṣibhiś cāhita-śakti-viśeṣaḥ*  
*śri-bhagavatā samam ātma-sambandha-viśeṣa-pratipādakāś ca \|*  
*tatra kevalāni śri-bhagavan-nāmāny api nirapekṣāṇy eva parama-*  
*puruṣartha-phala-paryanta-dāna-samarthāni*

**Although mantras are composed of the Supreme Lord’s names, they are distinct from** ***nāma*** **because they include the word** ***namaḥ*** **or** ***svāhā*****. They receive their special powers from the** ***ṛṣis*** **or from the Lord Himself. The holy names of the Lord, however, can directly bestow** ***kṛṣṇa-prema*****, being independent. Thus the names of Kṛṣṇa are more suitable than mantras.**

Although chanting the Lord’s holy names is the ultimate means of spiritual perfection, the *gāyatri-mantras* still have great significance in an aspirant’s life. One reason is that they have great power to liberate one from material conditioning, thus enabling one to chant the holy name purely. But even more important is their ability to focus one’s attention on Kṛṣṇa. As already discussed, these divine mantras are not different from their presiding Deities. By fulfilling one’s vow to chant the *gāyatri-mantras* three times daily with proper attention, one invokes the mercy of those Deities. By receiving Their mercy, one will begin to realize Their transcendental forms. Śrila Prabhupāda hints at that point in the following statement:

**Our present senses are all made of material elements, and therefore they are imperfect in realizing the transcendental form of Lord Viṣṇu. He is therefore worshiped by sound representation via the transcendental method of chanting. \[…\] The transcendental form of eternal bliss and knowledge can be experienced by our original spiritual senses, which can be revived by chanting of the holy mantras, or transcendental sound representations. \[…\] That will gradually lead us nearer to the Lord.**

We should consider the power inherent in meditating daily on the various forms of the Lord present in those mantras – and how the Lord glances mercifully upon any sincere *sādhaka* who tries to worship Him through the proper recitation of the divine *gāyatri-mantras*. In due course of time the Lord will surely be pleased to reveal that *sādhaka’s* relationship with Him, along with the activities and services he or she is meant to perform. Finally, the Lord will bestow love of Godhead upon such a thirsty soul.

The *bija-mantras*, the seed syllables in the *gāyatri-mantras*, especially act as transcendental “sonic thunderbolts” to drive away material contamination and enable us to chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* without offense. Only when we can chant without offense will Kṛṣṇa’s holy name bear its transcendental fruit. We should be inspired to accept such help.

## 5. Bhāgavata-mārga and Pañcarātrika-vidhi

To clarify the connection between the holy name and the *gāyatri-mantras*, let us study the following quote from Śrila Prabhupāda:

**There are two different ways of approaching the Supreme Lord. One is called** ***bhāgavata-mārga*****, or the way of Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, and the other is called** ***pañcarātrika-vidhi*****.** ***Pañcarātrika-vidhi*** **is the method of temple worship, and** ***bhāgavata-vidhi*** **is the system of nine processes, which begin with hearing and chanting. The Kṛṣṇa conscious movement accepts both processes simultaneously and thus enables one to make steady progress on the path of realization of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.**

Chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra*, in other words, belongs primarily to the *bhāgavata-mārga*, and chanting the *gāyatri-mantras* belongs to the *pañcarātrika-vidhi*. These two processes work closely together to help the devotee advance in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. They can be compared with the two tracks of a railway: One track alone will be of no use, but combined the two tracks create a perfect path for the train to reach its goal. Śrila Prabhupāda has also noted this interdependency:

**According to** ***pañcarātrika*** **system, Nārada-pañcarātra, this worship of the Deity is there. And** ***bhāgavata-mārga*****, the path of Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, is simply** ***śravaṇaṁ kirtanam*****. \[…\] So both the vidhis, both the systems have been introduced. One will help the other. That is the way. \[…\] Both, combined together, it is very much helpful.** ***Bhāgavata-mārga*** **will help the** ***pañcarātrika-marga*****, or process, and the** ***pañcarātrika*** **process will help** ***bhāgavata*** **process. Both together is helpful.**

**Two circles**

The following example is often used to illustrate how *kṛṣṇa-nāma* and *kṛṣṇa-mantra* (the *gāyatri-mantras*) are related to one another:

Symbolically, *kṛṣṇa-nāma* and the *gāyatri-mantras* are like two concentric circles, *gāyatri* being the smaller one. *Kṛṣṇa-nāma* spreads throughout the whole creation of the Lord and extends Himself to living beings on all levels of spiritual qualification from the highest to the lowest. Thus everyone on every level of advancement can start to chant the holy name, and by the holy name can reach the highest abode, Goloka-dhāma – provided one has attained the level of *śuddha-nāma*, where one is free from all offenses against the holy name and chants with full love of Godhead. But for that end the *gāyatri-mantras* are helpful. Since *gāyatri* is a smaller circle, it doesn’t extend itself to the lowest material levels; before receiving the *gāyatri-mantras* one first has to develop a *brāhmaṇa’s* qualities, at least to a certain degree. It also doesn’t reach to the highest level in Kṛṣṇa’s spiritual realm. At one point, therefore, when the chanting of the *gāyatri-mantras* has brought the *sādhaka* to the transcendental level (Vaikuṇṭha), the holy name will become predominant in his spiritual practice and further elevate him to the highest level of pure *kṛṣṇa-prema.*

At the same time, however, although the function of the *gāyatri-mantras* seems to be fulfilled at a certain level of advancement, one should never stop chanting them. To underline this statement, Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi quotes the following verse from the *tantras*:

*siddha-mantro ’pi pūtātmā  tri-sandhyaṁ devam arcayet niyamenaika-sandhyaṁ vā japed aṣṭottaraṁ śatam*

**Even if one has perfected his mantra and is completely pure in heart, he should still worship the Lord at the three sandhyās, or junctures of the day, by the method prescribed. Or at least at one of these times he should do this worship, and 108 times he should chant** ***japa*** **of his mantra.**

The reason is that the *gāyatri-mantras* are filled with the names of the allmerciful Lord. This means that a devotee who advances to the platform of spontaneous loving service chants *gāyatri* from a different perspective, the perspective of overflowing love, as opposed to the beginner’s ritualistic worship. On this very confidential level, *gāyatri* is even considered a *mahā-mantra*. Chanted with proper conception, it will transfer the devotee to Goloka-Vṛndāvana after this life, where he or she will attain the spiritual body of an eternal associate of the Lord and render eternal devotional service.

In his Mantrārtha-dipikā, Śrila Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura offers more insight into the confidential viewpoint of *rāgānuga-bhakti* and the *gāyatri-mantras.* You will find this information on page 243.

**The glories of the Bhāgavata-mārga**

Since the subject of this book – the *gāyatri-mantras* – is part of *pañcarātrika-vidhi*, we are naturally emphasizing the subjects related to this system. At the same time, we should always be aware of the importance of the *bhāgavata-mārga*, the main thrust of Lord Caitanya’s teachings.

For clarity, let us study how the Hari-bhakti-vilāsa, which deals mainly with the *pañcarātrika* system, highlights the importance of the *bhāgavata-mārga*.

Traditionally the chanting of the *gāyatri-mantras* is accompanied by various procedures. They include *ācamana* (purifying with water), *prāṇāyāma* (breathing exercises), *saṅkalpa* (fixing the mind or intention on chanting), *dhyāna* (meditation), *arghya* (purifying the mouth), and so on. To chant the *gāyatri-mantras* in accordance with these procedures at the three junctions of the day is called *sandhyā-vandana*. A very advanced devotee, who has risen to the stage of spontaneous smaraṇa, or remembrance, may skip these practices and even stop chanting *gāyatri* altogether. The reason is that he has already reached the goal of all practice: constant absorption in the Lord. The Hari-bhakti-vilāsa explicitly approves of such an attitude. Although emphasizing the importance of the *sandhyā-vandana* rituals, it also states that one can leave them aside upon having come to the level of chanting the mantra without offense, with full attention, and with full concentration on its meaning:

*śri-kṛṣṇa-bhaktyā saktyā tu sandhyo pāsyādikaṁ yadi*  
*patet karma na pātitya doṣa-śaṅka kathañcana*

**If due to absorption in devotional service to Kṛṣṇa, one forgets to chant his promised** ***gāyatri-mantras*****, although usually by neglecting to chant them one would be considered fallen and at fault, still, in such circumstances there is no diminishment in one’s advancement in spiritual life.**

Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi explains why this is so:

**Certainly one has to chant his** ***gāyatri-mantras*** **to worship the Supreme Personality of Godhead. If someone forgets this, how can he worship the Supreme Personality of Godhead? No kinds of spiritual functions can be done by such a person. Here, however, it is written, “*****kṛṣṇa-bhakti*****,” which means “being enthusiastically attached to doing devotional service to Lord Śri Kṛṣṇa,” this nullifies all sins. In other words, because a devotee of Kṛṣṇa is very enthusiastic to serve Him, no kind of sin or falling down is attached to him.**

Our revered *ācārya*, Śrila Mādhavendra Puri, made the same point in a very personal way when he composed the following prayer:

*sandhyā-vandana bhadram astu bhavato bhoḥ snāna tubhyaṁ namo*  
*bho devāḥ pitaraś ca tarpaṇa-vidhau nāhaṁ kṣamaḥ kṣamyatām*  
*yatra kvāpi niṣadya yādava-kulottamasya kaṁsa-dviṣaḥsmār*  
*aṁ smāram aghaṁ harāmi tad alaṁ manye kim anyena me*

**O my prayers three times a day \[*****sandhyā-vandana*****\], all glory to you.**  
**O bathing, I offer my obeisances unto you. O demigods! O forefathers! Please excuse me for my inability to offer you my respects. Now wherever I sit, I can remember the great descendant of the Yadu dynasty, Kṛṣṇa, the enemy of Kaṁsa, and thereby I can free myself from all sinful bondage. I think this is sufficient for me.**

Here we find a great devotee who for the sake of performing spontaneous devotional service renounced the regulated practice of chanting his *gāyatri-mantras*. Such statements are typical of those on the *bhāgavata-mārga*, the path that concentrates entirely on the Supreme Lord’s pleasure. Caitanya Mahāprabhu lived and preached this path, and it is the treasure of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

**Mantra leads to** ***nāma***

An interesting and enlivening example of how these two processes work together is found in the Bṛhad-Bhāgavatāmṛta. There, we hear of an uneducated *brāhmaṇa* from Prāgjyotiṣa. Although born as a *brāhmaṇa*, he had never properly studied the scriptures nor understood their spiritual conclusions from the explanations of authorized teachers. His heart remained full of material desire. But because he was a faithful worshiper of the goddess Kāmākhyā, a form of Lord Siva’s wife Parvati, the goddess became pleased with him and out of kindness appeared in a dream. She revealed the *gopāla-mantra* to him and provided instructions on how to chant it and perform the various details of practice.

As Sanātana Gosvāmi explains, although this brāhmaṇa was an unfortunate soul, the *gopāla-mantra* made such a vivid impression on him that he completely forgot his other religious activities. Just by chanting this mantra he gradually gave up his desire for wealth and became interested in spiritual subjects. Indeed, his spiritual search became the most important endeavor in his life. Impelled by the mantra he gave up his residence and underwent a long pilgrimage, living as a mendicant. On his journey he visited a number of places and was gradually introduced to the various concepts of Vedic life. He learned to perform fruitive activities, which elevate one to the heavenly planets, and how to attain liberation by practicing the path of *jñāna*. Out of respect for the goddess Kāmākhyā, however, he never stopped chanting the mantra he had received from her. Then one night in a dream, he saw the mantra’s Deity, the most charming Lord Madana-gopāla.

Through this experience and by the mantra’s power, his heart became almost completely purified. In another dream, Lord Siva and his wife Parvati – whom the brāhmaṇa worshiped as goddess Kāmākhyā – appeared and advised him to go to Vraja, where he would receive further instructions.

On his way to Vraja he passed through Prayāga, where he proved his ignorance in transcendental matters when he met devotees of Kṛṣṇa performing *nāma-saṅkirtana*. Thinking them ordinary stage performers, he disrespectfully interrupted their kirtana.

Still, by faithfully chanting his mantra at the proper times, he advanced more and more, up to the stage where he sometimes had the feeling that he was seeing the Lord directly standing in front of him. Finally, he arrived in Vraja, where he met his *guru,* Gopa-kumāra, the Lord’s personal associate. Appearing before the *brāhmaṇa* as a young cowherd boy, he was absorbed in chanting the Lord’s names in pure *prema*. Considering this young child of the cowherds “the best of wise men and a great saint,” the *brāhmaṇa* submissively inquired from him about the goal he should strive for and the means to reach it: “Whatever the goddess ordered me,” he said, “I regularly do. But I have no idea about the results of those duties, or even what kind of acts they are. I therefore think my life worthless, and simply want to die.”

Hearing the *brāhmaṇa’s* pitiful words, Gopa-kumāra thought:

*etasya kṛta-kṛtyasya jātā pūrṇārthatā kila*  
*kevalaṁ tat-padāmbhoja- sākṣād-ikṣāvaśiṣyate*  
*taj-jape ’rhati nāsaktiṁ kintu tan-nāma-kirtane*

**This person has done everything he needs to do. Indeed, his life is a complete success. All that remains for him to achieve is to see directly the lotus feet of the Lord. He needs to become attached not so much to the solitary chanting of the Lord’s mantra (*****taj-jape*****) but to the** ***saṅkirtana*** **of His names (*****tan-nāma-kirtane*****).**

This brings us back to our discussion. Chanting the mantra – *pañcarātrika-vidhi* – is not enough. Although this process had brought the *brāhmaṇa* to almost full success, he now needed to learn about the *saṅkirtana* of the holy name – the *bhāgavata-mārga*. His Grace Gopiparāṇadhana Prabhu paraphrases Sanātana Gosvāmi in his commentary to the above verse:

**When received by** ***pañcarātrika*** **initiation in a** ***vaiṣṇava-sampradāya*****,** ***viṣṇu-mantras*** **deliver the chanter from illusion and provide him liberation and entrance into the divine life of devotional service. But the highest standard of pure love of God is obtainable not by the** ***pañcarātrika-vidhi*** **but by the** ***bhāgavata-mārga*** **of** ***nāma-saṅkirtana*****, worship of Kṛṣṇa’s names without material motives. The Prāgjy-otiṣa** ***brāhmaṇa*** **had been chanting the ten-syllable** ***gopāla-mantra*** **for some time with good effect, but only after he heard the** ***śuddha-nāma*** **from Gopa-kumāra was he able to chant the same names of Lord Gopāla in the perfect consciousness of** ***saṅkirtana*****. Offenseless saṅkirtana with concentrated attention naturally develops into spontaneous attraction to Kṛṣṇa and His loving service.**

**The power of Pañcarātra**

In considering the Prāgjyotiṣa *brāhmaṇa’s* experience, we should note that the cause of his advancement was not his own understanding but the transcendental influence exerted by the mantra. Śrila Prabhupāda once compared the effect of mantras to thunder. We do not need to intellectually understand what is happening when the sound of thunder fills the sky; the sound of thunder affects our consciousness and we are moved by it. In the same way, even without understanding what he was doing, the brāhmaṇa attained a high level of advancement. This is the power of the *pañcarātrika* method.

Yet the *brāhmaṇa* remained confused about the actual goal of life. He needed to develop attachment to the Lord by practicing *nāma-saṅkirtana.* In order to return to the spiritual world, a devotee needs to come to the stage of pure love of God. *Prema* can only be achieved by practicing the activities of the *bhāgavata-mārga*, by *nāma-saṅkirtana*. Therefore, Gopa-kumāra thought:

*śriman-madana-gopāla- pādābjopāsanāt param nāma-saṅkirtana-prāyād vāñchātita-phala-pradāt*  
*tal-lilā-sthala-palināṁ śraddhā-sandarśanādaraiḥ sampadyamānān nitaraṁ kiñcin nāsty eva sādhanam*

**Certainly no** ***sādhana*** **\[means of perfection\] is superior to the worship of Śriman Madana-gopāla’s lotus feet. This worship yields results far beyond what one could expect. It should be performed mainly through** ***nāma-saṅkirtana,*** **along with reverence and affection for the many places of Śri Gopāla’s pastimes, places one should regularly visit.**

Let us reflect for a moment: How had the *brāhmaṇa* been able to rise to such a high spiritual level even before meeting Gopa-kumāra? It is because he had already worshiped Śri Kṛṣṇa through his chanting of the *gopāla-mantra* – even if his chanting was irregular and without understanding. Thus he achieved a certain level of advancement without even realizing it. Now, however, in order to come close to Kṛṣṇa, he needed the special mercy of a pure devotee, someone who would instruct him how to conquer Kṛṣṇa’s heart. He needed to learn how to chant Śri Kṛṣṇa’s holy names, following the methods prescribed by the *bhāgavata-mārga*.

But only after having chanted the *gopāla-mantra* – in other words, by following *pañcarātrika-vidhi* – was the *brāhmaṇa* prepared for the step up to the *bhāgavata-mārga*. We should seriously consider this point. Although even *nāmābhāsa* chanting – characterized by a genuine effort to give up offenses – will help us advance quickly in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, only chanting the holy name purely will yield the fruit of pure love of God. A devotee who has received the *gāyatri-mantras* should always remember that these mantras, if chanted properly, can reveal knowledge of his particular relationship with Kṛṣṇa and of the mood in which he should worship the Lord. Only then can he actually rise to the platform of pure chanting. Śrila Prabhupāda summarizes this point in the following statement:

**No one can worship the Lord in the** ***bhāgavata-vidhi*** **without going through the regulations of the** ***pañcarātrika-vidhi*****. Actually, neophyte devotees worship the Lord according to the** ***pañcarātrika-vidhi*****, or the regulative principles enjoined in the Nārada-pañcarātra. \[…\] Worship according to the** ***pañcarātrika-vidhi*** **is called** ***vidhi-mārga*****, and worship according to the** ***bhāgavata-vidhi*** **principles is called** ***rāga-mārga*****. The principles of** ***rāga-mārga*** **are especially meant for devotees who are elevated to the Vṛndāvana platform.**

## 6. The five saṁskāras in pañcarātrika-vidhi

In conclusion, the process of spiritual life is aimed at transforming false identity into the true identity of eternal servitude to Kṛṣṇa. To achieve that goal, the *pañcarātra* enumerates five *saṁskāras*, or essential sacraments, for spiritual initiation. The Padma Purāṇa describes them as follows:

*tāpaḥ puṇdras tathā nāma mantro yāgaś ca pañcamaḥ*  
*ami hi panca-saṁskārāḥ  paramaikānta-hetavaḥ*

**The five purifying processes (*****saṁskāras*****) that lead to single-minded devotion are:**

1. ***tāpa*** **practicing prescribed austerities (such as the four rules and regulations)**
2. ***puṇdra*** **decorating the body with the tilaka markings (appropriate for one’s particular disciplic succession)**
3. ***nāma*** **chanting the holy name**
4. ***mantra*** **chanting the gāyatri-mantras**
5. ***yāga*** **performing sacrifice for the sake of Viṣṇu \[Deity worship\].**

Śrila Bhaktivinoda Thākura explains that this system is part of the ten *saṁskāras*, or purifying processes, that accompany a human being through life, beginning with *garbhadāna*, the conception rituals, and ending with antyeṣṭi, the cremation rites. One of these ten *saṁskāras* is initiation, in which the above five items are given to the disciple.

Initiation is complete only when the above five elements are intact because these saṁskāras have the potency to eradicate bad karma and lead us forward on the progressive spiritual path. According to Bhaktivinoda Thākura, conditioned souls cannot develop intense devotion to Lord Kṛṣṇa unless they have accepted these five *saṁskāras*. Let us therefore study them one by one:

**1.** ***Tāpa*** **– Accepting Austerity**

When students have received initial spiritual knowledge, they come to understand that the material world is not their permanent home and that it is not worth investing more energy here than required to keep body and soul together. Such students become aware that they must take shelter of a guru in order to obtain the Lord.

The spiritual master’s duty is to study the students who approach him for some time to see if their sentiments are real or fleeting. When the guru recognizes that an aspiring disciple has developed a healthy aversion to material life, he will begin to train him or her. Only a sincere disciple will be prepared to accept serious *tāpa* and be freed from the bondage of material attachment.

The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement asks a prospective disciple to follow the four rules and to accept *sādhana* focused on *śravaṇam*, *kirtanam*, and service to *guru* and the Deities. Without such regulation, the Kṛṣṇa conscious process will not work. Śrila Prabhupāda says:

**Of course, this chanting of hari-nāma will make you purified. That’s nice. But just like this fire I am going to ignite. This is dry firewood. But if I help it, to keep it dry, then the fire will be very nice, blazing fire. But if I pour water on it, then it will be difficult to ignite. \[…\] Similarly, Kṛṣṇa consciousness or chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa will keep you progressive. At the same time, voluntarily, if you do not commit all this nonsense, then it will be very nice. And if you continue this water pouring, then…**

*Tāpa* does not refer only to external cleansing. It also means to clean the heart. This is an active endeavor, brought about when one repents one’s previous ignorant and sinful activities and develops a genuine detachment from materialistic living. Ajāmila’s prayers provide a good model for study in this regard.

The word *tāpa* has an interesting literal meaning. It is derived from the verbal root tap, “to burn.” In previous times disciples would brand their bodies with the symbols of Viṣṇu just as farmers brand their cattle. Branding signifies ownership, and by branding themselves devotees showed that they now considered themselves the Lord’s property. The Gaudiya-Vaiṣṇavas never adopted this custom; instead, they write the Lord’s holy names on their body, usually with sacred clay.

**2.** ***Puṇdra*** **– Applying Tilaka**

*Puṇdra* refers to the *tilaka* markings symbolizing Lord Viṣṇu that Vaiṣṇavas apply. The more specific word is *ūrdhva-puṇdra*. It distinguishes the *vaiṣṇava-tilaka* mark, which is upright (*ūrdhva*), from the horizontal mark of Lord Siva’s worshipers. When devotees mark their body with *vaiṣṇava-tilaka* they show that they consider the body a temple of the Lord rather than their own property. As the Padma Purāṇa explains:

**Persons who put Tulasi beads on their neck, who mark twelve places of their bodies as Viṣṇu temples with Viṣṇu’s symbolic representations, and who have** ***viṣṇu-tilaka*** **on their foreheads, are to be understood as the devotees of Lord Viṣṇu in this world. Their presence purifies the world, and anywhere they remain, they make that place as good as Vaikuṇṭha.**

As with *tāpa*, *ūrdhva-puṇdra* has an internal aspect: taking full shelter of the path of surrender that brings the soul back to Godhead. This path, which enlightens one’s whole existence, consists of engaging body, mind, and soul in Kṛṣṇa’s service with attachment to His lotus feet. Śrila Prabhupāda stated this principle in his own unique way:

**We have to follow. Just like we have got** ***tilaka.*** **If you say, “Why this** ***tilaka*****?” there is no question of “why.”** ***Mahājano yena gataḥ sa panthaḥ*****. We have been instructed by the** ***ācāryas*****; we have to follow that. That is surrender.**

Śrila Bhaktivinoda Thākura also stressed that *ūrdhva-puṇdra* is far more than an external decoration. Rather, it is a sign of self-surrender and should be done in a meditative mood:

**O repentant soul, don’t delay! Mark the body, mind, and soul with** ***ūrdhva-puṇdra*** **and follow the path of advancement, which leads you to the kingdom of God.**

**3.** ***Nāma*** **– Receiving and Chanting the Holy Name**

At first initiation (technically called *nāma-dikṣā*), the spiritual master offers his disciple the Lord’s holy names. These names will awaken the soul’s eternal nature. When the disciple is able to avoid the ten offenses and chant with deep attachment and longing, he will taste the Lord’s personal association as if he were tasting pure nectar. The holy name will carry him to the supreme abode, Goloka Vṛndāvana.

One achieves the perfection of chanting when his mind always remembers the name, the intelligence always thinks the name, and the soul always adores the name.

*Nāma* can also refer to the name the *guru* bestows on his disciple, causing him to think, “I am now a *hari-dāsa*, not an enjoyer. Even *māyā* is eternally connected with Lord Hari. I must therefore engage her in Lord Hari’s service.” This is called the *nāma-karaṇa* (name-giving) ceremony, and it is a necessary part of the initiation process.

The Caitanya-caritāmṛta contains an endearing description of how Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu conducted a *nāma-karaṇa* ceremony. This incident happened when the two brothers Dabira Khasa and Sākara Mallika, who became Rūpa and Sanātana Gosvāmis, met Caitanya Mahāprabhu at Rāmakeli:

**After hearing the prayer of Dabira Khasa and Sākara Mallika, Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu said, “My dear Dabira Khasa, you two brothers are My old servants. My dear Sākara Mallika, from this day your names will be changed to Śrila Rūpa and Śrila Sanātana.”**

Śrila Prabhupāda comments:

Actually this is Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s initiation of Dabira Khasa and Sākara Mallika. They approached the Lord with all humility, and the Lord accepted them as old servants, as eternal servants, and He changed their names. It is to be understood from this that it is essential for a disciple to change his name after initiation.

*śaṅkha-cakrādy-ūrdhva-puṇdra- dhāraṇādy-ātma-lakṣaṇam*  
*tan nāma-karaṇaṁ caiva vaiṣṇavatvam ihocyate*

**After initiation, the disciple’s name must be changed to indicate that he is a servant of Lord Viṣṇu. The disciple should also immediately begin marking his body with** ***tilaka*** **(*****ūrdhva-puṇdra*****), especially his forehead. These are spiritual marks, symptoms of a perfect Vaiṣṇava.**

**4.** ***Mantra*** **– Chanting Gāyatri**

At second initiation (technically called *mantra-dikṣā*), when a disciple has shown his or her brahminical qualification or at least a deep interest in developing such qualification, the spiritual master bestows the *gāyatri-mantras,* especially the *gopāla-mantra* and the *kāma-gāyatri.*

A mantra is a prayer that addresses God by His names in the dative case. In Sanskrit, the dative case indicates to whom we are offering our meditation. It can be recognized by the ending -*āya* (*kṛṣṇāya*, *vasudevāya*, etc.). You will find more information on this topic on page 288.

The *gāyatri-mantras* help the disciple to develop a higher taste for the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* by pointing out more details about Lord Kṛṣṇa, the person being invoked by the name, and of the relationship between Kṛṣṇa and the individual soul. As already explained, these mantras also include powerful *bija-mantras.* When chanted properly, these *bija-mantras* will destroy old sinful tendencies.

**5.** ***Yāga*** **– Performing Sacrifice**

*Yāga* (or *yajña*) is the procedure of worshiping the Lord by engaging the physical and mental faculties in worshiping the *arca-vigraha* and the *śālagrāma-śilā*. *Yāga* can only be properly performed when one has received the sacred mantras. One’s mind and senses are fully engaged when one performs Deity worship (*yajña*). Deity worship engages one’s actions, discrimination, thoughts, and sensory experiences, and connects them with the Lord. In this way, one undergoes an all-embracing purification that even removes one’s *karma*.

**Conclusion**

If we study these five *saṁskāras* their purpose will become clear: they allow for a new birth by which the disciple can start his or her journey to the spiritual world. Their first function is to free the disciple from material entanglement. The scriptures say that if one practices devotional service, especially chanting the holy name, one can become free even from his *prārabdha-karma* (manifest sinful reactions).

These *pañca-saṁskāras* are important tools for devotees desiring to make spiritual advancement. By carefully studying Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi’s books, especially his Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu, we can see that Rūpa Gosvāmi has meticulously embedded the *pañca-saṁskāras* in his system of *sādhana*. We find them, for example, reflected in his sixty-four principles of devotional service. Because of this, Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi is renowned as our *abhidheya-ācārya*, the teacher of the process of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Since Śrila Prabhupāda emphasized the methods Rūpa Gosvāmi prescribed, the *pañca-saṁskāras* are also included in his teachings, although he did not explicitly mention them as they appear in the Padma Purāṇa. The reason I have discussed them here in such detail is to help us gain an understanding of how closely our Kṛṣṇa conscious practices are connected to the *pañcarātrika* system.

Please study The Nectar of Devotion in this regard, especially Chapters 6-13, and appreciate how accurately and wonderfully Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi has designed the Kṛṣṇa conscious process for the upliftment of our eternal soul in this fallen age, utilizing both the principles of the *bhāgavata-mārga* and the *pañcarātrika-vidhi*.

## II. Preparing the ground

## 1. Realization according to meditation

The *gāyatri-mantras*, especially the *brahma-gāyatri*, are a common subject in almost all spiritual schools, temples, and āśramas both inside and outside India. Thus there are many interpretations, opinions, explanations, and methods on how to chant them. Before going to the practical part of this book, it therefore seems necessary to orient ourselves by establishing our own spiritual tradition’s viewpoint.

Before beginning any activity, it is important to understand the goal of the activity and the means to attain it. Without this knowledge, where will we find ourselves? Śrila Prabhupāda gives a simple analogy about rowing a boat: If we lose sight of the goal, we will row in circles and not go anywhere. Similarly, while chanting the *gāyatri-mantras*, it is essential to know both the *sādhya* (goal) and the *sādhana* (methods and regulations to attain the goal).

While internally reciting the *gāyatri-mantras*, the devotee should be aware who among God’s many forms is being addressed. In his prayers to Śri Viṣṇu for creative energy, Lord Brahmā describes how the Lord reveals Himself according to His devotee’s meditation:

*yad-yad-dhiyā ta urugāya vibhāvayanti*  
*tat-tad-vapuḥ praṇayase sad-anugrahāya*

**O my Lord, You are so merciful to Your devotees that You manifest Yourself in the particular eternal form of transcendence in which they always think of You.**

This statement can be summarized by the formula “realization according to meditation.” Śrila Prabhupāda writes in his purport to the above verse:

The statement here that the Lord manifests Himself before the devotee in the form in which the devotee likes to worship Him, indicates that the Lord becomes subordinate to the desire of the devotee – so much so that He manifests His particular form as the devotee demands.

Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu teaches the same point to Veṅkaṭa Bhaṭṭa:

*eka iśvara – bhaktera dhyāna-anurūpa*  
*eka-i vigrahe kare nānākāra rūpa*
