Śrila Bhaktivinoda Thākura points out that if while engaged in glorifying the Lord the proper time for chanting *gāyatri* passes, it is not offensive to let it pass. Why? Because glorification of the Lord is the essence of *gāyatri-japa*. Devotees should still chant the mantras at a convenient time and should not skip them. And of course Bhaktivinoda Thākura’s words do not provide an excuse for missing the *sandhyās* due to indifference or inattention.

If one should fail to chant his *gāyatri-mantras* in the morning, he should not try to chant them later, before noon, but should wait for the noon *sandhyā* and then chant two sets of mantras. In the same way, if one is unable to chant both morning and noon *gayatris*, he should wait for the evening *sandhyā* and then chant three sets of mantras. If, however, unusual circumstances make concentrated *gāyatri* chanting either difficult or impossible, one should use his discretion to temporarily adjust his *gāyatri-japa* according to time, place, and circumstances.

For one who finds the information in this section too complicated, it might be best to stick to Śrila Prabhupāda’s advice to those with active devotional lives or who are forced by circumstances they cannot control: chant the mantras at 6:00 A.M., 12:00 P.M., and 6:00 P.M.

Śrila Prabhupāda himself tried to maintain a disciplined routine when chanting *gāyatri*. Hari Śauri Prabhu, one of his personal servants, relates:

**In the morning just before sunrise, Prabhupāda would finish translating, wash his face, apply fresh** ***tilaka*** **and chant** ***gāyatri*****. Then he would go for a morning walk. His noon** ***gāyatri*** **would be chanted at about 1:15 P.M. after bathing, dressing in fresh clothes and applying** ***tilaka*****. He would chant his evening** ***gāyatri*** **sometimes after taking** ***darśana*** **of Śri Śri Rādhā-Mādhava. I would often chant** ***gāyatri*** **with Śrila Prabhupāda. He would sit in his** ***darśana*** **room or on a seat, keep his eyes open and chant his** ***gāyatri*** **in about 4 or 5 minutes.**

**When not to chant**

It is important to remember that all devotional activities, including the chanting of *gāyatri-mantras*, are meant solely to please the Supreme Personality of Godhead. And since the *gāyatri-mantras* are a way to address the Deities personally, they should be chanted when the Deities are awake. Since They are resting between 9:30 P.M. and 3:30 A.M., we should avoid *mantra-japa* during that time. The same applies to Their midday rest, which in most temples falls between 1:30 and 3:30 P.M. In both cases, these hours fall after the *sandhyās* and are therefore not proper times to chant the mantras.

One should also not chant *gāyatri* when the external circumstances are too disturbing and do not allow for peaceful, absorbed meditation.

**How to chant**

The word *japa* comes from the verb *jap*, “to utter or whisper.” Kṛṣṇa says in Bhagavad-gitā that of sacrifices He is *japa*. Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi describes *japa* in the following words:

*Japa-yajña* is of three kinds, as stated in the Nṛsiṁha Purāṇa:

1\. *vācika*, verbal  
2\. *upāṁśu*, whispered  
3\. *mānasika*, within the mind

Each method is successively better than the previous one. When a mantra is pronounced distinctly, either in a high, low, or resonant voice, it is known as *vācika-japa*. When a mantra is uttered slowly with a slight movement of the lips, and can be heard only by one’s own ears, it is known as *upāṁśu-japa*. When one meditates on the meaning of the mantra by applying the intelligence, going repeatedly from one syllable to the next, from one word to the next, it is known as *mānasika-japa*. According to the sage Yājñavalkya, *upāṁśu-japa* is one hundred times superior to *vācika-japa,* and *mānasika-japa* is one thousand times superior to *vācika-japa*. This is because *mānasika-japa* is equivalent to *dhyāna*, or deep meditation.

How then should we chant? Since mānasika-japa is considered superior to vācika and upāṁśu, should we chant our gāyatri-mantras only in the mind? Śrila Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Thākura seems to support this idea in his explanation of the nature of mantras:

**A mantra is an inaudibly recited, liberating, self-dedicatory, transcendental sound.**

“Inaudibly recited” means chanted in the mind. Are we meant to chant *gāyatri* mentally? Ideally, yes. We have to consider, however, that nowadays only those with purified and peaceful minds can maintain concentration on the *mānasika* or mental recitation of mantras. The atmosphere in which we live is highly disturbed, and our “internal atmosphere” is often shaken by outside influences. Thus Śrila Prabhupāda instructed us to whisper the mantras instead, as he wrote in a letter to one disciple:

**The** ***gāyatri-mantra*** **is not to be said loudly.** ***Gāyatri*** **means to sing, but singing can be done loudly or very softly. The** ***gāyatri-mantra*** **should be sung in a low whispering voice.**

To another disciple he wrote:

**I am so glad to learn that both of you are now chanting 16 rounds or more regularly and murmuring the** ***gāyatri-mantras*** **regularly. Please continue this habit without fail.**

The following quote, in which Śrila Prabhupāda refers to the *gopāla-gāyatri-mantra*, underlines the same point:

**There is no difference between meditating and chanting, but in the present age meditation is not possible on this planet. Therefore loud chanting of a mantra like the** ***mahā-mantra*****, Hare Kṛṣṇa, with soft chanting of the** ***aṣṭadaśākṣara*****, the mantra of eighteen syllables, is recommended.**

Śrila Prabhupāda seemed to himself prefer to whisper the mantras softly:

**After bathing, dressing, and applying** ***tilaka*****, Prabhupāda would silently recite the** ***gāyatri-mantras*****. Holding his** ***brāhmaṇa*** **thread and looping it around his right thumb, he would sit erect, silently moving his lips.**

I think it is important to pay attention to this point. We will derive little benefit from mentally reciting the sacred *gāyatri-mantras* without conscientiously meditating on them. From the example of the *ācāryas* as well as from the experiences of many practitioners, we can conclude that nowadays it is easier to be fully attentive while whispering the mantras. The point is to be concentrated! Let us try to chant *gāyatri-japa* with an awakened heart, with loving emotions.

There are two ways to practice *upāṁśu-japa.* One is to move the lips while remaining silent, and the second is to murmur or make a whispering sound. We can use either of these ways according to our preference. There may indeed be rare situations in which we are perfectly concentrated and peaceful and may prefer to chant mentally, but for most of us, such times will be the exception.

When Śrila Prabhupāda was in Australia, William, a professional film producer, had the opportunity to film him while chanting his *gāyatri- mantras.* Kūrma Prabhu, the author of The Great Transcendental Adventure, describes this instructive scene:

**William zoomed in close, just as Prabhupāda, facing the picture of his spiritual master, began silently reciting the** ***gāyatri-mantras*****. William captured on film Prabhupāda’s methodical, aristocratic yet delicate movements. Holding his sacred** ***brāhmaṇa*** **thread and looping it around his right thumb, he sat erect, looking slightly downwards, moving his lips a little.**

**Counting the mantras**

Each line of the *gāyatri-mantras* must be chanted ten times. This is the direction of Śrila Prabhupāda. Other *vaiṣṇavas*, including some *gaudiya-vaiṣṇavas*, chant each mantra twenty times or more. In South India, some *brāhmaṇas* mainly chant the *gāyatri-mantras* and only rarely chant the *mahā-mantra*. Therefore they chant each mantra 108, 1008, or even more times. As Śrila Prabhupāda’s disciples and followers, of course, we follow his *bhajana-praṇāli* (instruction on *bhajana*).

In order to free the mind for meditation, the mantras are counted by the thumb of the right hand. With each mantra the thumb moves from one finger limb to the next. Start with the middle limb of the ring finger, move to its first limb, and turn clockwise up to the first limb of the index finger. Alternatively, the finger joints can be used for counting, in the same sequence. For more specific instructions, ask any initiated *brāhmaṇa*.

Since the mantras are confidential we are recommended to chant them unobtrusively. That is, we should not allow others to see that we are chanting. Therefore, the hand used for counting the mantras should be covered.

***Upavita*** **– the sacred thread**

At the time of second initiation, men receive from their guru a sacred thread called the *yajñopavita* (or simply, *upavita*). *Yajñopavita* literally means “that which surrounds *Viṣṇu*” (*yajña* – Viṣṇu; *upa-vi-ita* – surrounding). The idea is that a *brāhmaṇa* wears a thread that surrounds Lord Viṣṇu. Śrila Prabhupāda writes:

**The sacred thread is a sign of those who are competent to study the Vedas from the** ***ācārya*** **or the bona fide spiritual master. \[…\] The spiritual master accepts only the sincere inquirer as his disciple and gives him the sacred thread.**

Devotees often wonder how many single strands the upavita should have. The brāhmaṇas in Indian culture usually wear nine threads. According to some *vaiṣṇavas*, these nine threads symbolize the nine processes of *bhakti* (*śravaṇam*, *kirtanam*, etc.). Others say they refer to the nine islands of Śri Navadvipa-dhāma, and yet others say they represent the nine stages from *śraddha* to *prema*, as explained in Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu. (Therefore, some Vaiṣṇavas have explained, these three ideas correspond to one another.)

Śrila Prabhupāda wore three times three threads when he was placed in *samādhi*. Other *vaiṣṇavas* in the Gaudiya tradition wear six single threads to signify the six Gosvāmis. From this we can conclude that the exact number of threads is ultimately not so important.

More important is that the upavita is kept clean. Also, it should never be taken off until it has become too old and worn and must be changed. This is a sign of respect.

While receiving a massage with oil, shaving, or performing any activity that would dirty the *upavita*, one can bind it around his waist. As soon as one returns to his normal activities, the thread must be restored to its original position, hanging from the left shoulder and down the right side.

While using the toilet, the thread should be wrapped around the right ear. Since it is understood that all the holy tirthas reside in the right ear, the thread remains pure while in that position, even when the rest of the body becomes impure.

**Proper place**

The ideal place to chant the *gāyatri-mantras* is before the Deities, Tulasi Devi, or Śrila Prabhupāda, in the presence of one’s own *guru*, or where other *brāhmaṇas* are chanting. The scriptures state in this regard:

*siddha-kṣetreṣu tirtheṣu devatānāñca-sannidhau*  
*sahasraṁ śata-koṭinām anantaṁ viṣṇu-sannidhau*

**If one chants his** ***japa*** **in a transcendental place, or a place of pilgrimage, or near the place of demigods \[like Lord Śiva\], he receives results multiplied by 1,000,000,000. And if one chants near the Deity of Lord Viṣṇu or Kṛṣṇa, he achieves unlimited results.**

If one is not at the temple one should face east during the morning and noon *sandhyās* and north during the evening *sandhyā*. Each direction is favorable for a different reason. The east is favorable for the performance of devotion, the south for the performance of *karma* (fruitive activities), the west for the performance of *jñāna* (mental speculation), and the north for *yoga*. Of course, if one is in the presence of the Deity or the guru, facing them takes precedence over compass directions. (For ceremonial purposes the direction of the Deity is often considered east.)

It is especially conducive to chant *gāyatri* while standing waist-deep in a holy river, beside an ocean, or on a mountain peak. The scriptures recommend that we do not chant in moving vehicles, on a bed, or with the back toward the temple, the Deities, the fire, a holy tree, or a body of water such as a river or lake.

**Bodily posture**

In Bhagavad-gitā Kṛṣṇa provides useful instructions for yoga practitioners:

*samaṁ kāya-śiro-grivaṁ dhārayann acalaṁ sthiraḥ*  
*samprekṣya nāsikāgraṁ svaṁ diśaś cānavalokayan*

**One should hold one’s body, neck and head erect in a straight line and stare steadily at the tip of the nose.**

**This instruction has become common knowledge in the meditational disciplines of all cultures at all times. When the back is straight, the nervous system is aligned and it is easier to concentrate. In this connection, the Hari-bhakti-vilāsa instructs, “When chanting the** ***gāyatri-mantras*** **the** ***sādhaka*** **should not move his head and neck about, nor show his teeth.”**

Also, chanting *mantra-japa* is a *yajña*; we are meant to be worshiping the Lord (*yajñānāṁ japa-yajño ’smi*). Therefore, we should always sit on a mat while chanting, rather than on the bare floor, since the latter is counted as an offense against the Deity. This is confirmed by the following statement:

*anāsanaḥ śayāno vā gacchann utthita eva vā*  
*rathyāyāma śive sthāne na japet timirāntare*  
*upānad-gudha-pādo vā yāna-śayyāgatas tathā*  
*prāsarya na japet padav utkaṭāsana eva va*

**One should not chant** ***japa*** **sitting without a seat, while lying down sleeping, while walking, while standing, while on a path or in an impure place such as a crematorium, or while in dark places. While wearing shoes, riding on a carrier, laying down, or spreading the legs, one should not chant** ***japa*****.**

## 5. Fixing the mind

If we want to enliven our *gāyatri-japa*, the internal or mental attitude is even more important than the external rules we have introduced so far. “Devotee means the mind,” Śrila Prabhupāda said:

**To stick to this principle, to apply the mind always in Kṛṣṇa, that means he’s devotee. This is devotee. Devotee means not with four hands or four legs, no. The hands, legs are the same. But his mode of thinking is different. That’s all. That is devotee. Devotee does not depend on the country, color or religion or circumstance. Devotee means the mind. Anyone who is always thinking of Kṛṣṇa, he is devotee. That is the first qualification of devotee.** ***Man-manā bhāva mad-bhakto*****. Therefore we have to practice.**

One important way to think of Kṛṣṇa is that while chanting the *gāyatri-mantras* one sincerely endeavors to absorb the mind in their meaning. Here is Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi’s advice:

**One should sanctify one’s mind by withdrawing it from the objects of the senses and by silently meditating upon the meaning of the mantra. If the mantra remains within the mind and the mind remains within the mantra \[the mantra and mind must be connected\], then the characteristics of** ***gāyatri-japa*** **are revealed to the chanter.**

When trying to establish the proper mental attitude, it is also essential to become fully aware of the personality being worshiped by each mantra and to offer Him our full attention. In the next two chapters we will dive deeply into the vast ocean of the mantras’ meanings by systematically examining each word and syllable.

**Gaining the proper perspective**

As we discussed in the last chapter, the *pañcarātrika-vidhi* or path of rules and regulations given by authorities is extremely helpful to us. It is yet very important to understand the underlying purpose of these rules and regulations. All the rules and regulations are meant to help us remember Kṛṣṇa and never forget Him. In regard to chanting the *gāyatri-mantras* this means that we should worship Kṛṣṇa as the *mantra-devata.* When one adheres to chanting *gāyatri* punctually (during the *sandhyās*), the presiding Deity of the respective *sandhyā*, being pleased with our sincere *sādhana*, will help direct our meditation toward Kṛṣṇa. In this way, Kṛṣṇa Himself becomes pleased with our love and devotion and helps us develop *smaraṇa*. The following verse explains what our attitude should be while performing activities according to the rules and regulations, including *gāyatri-japa*:

*smartavyaḥ satataṁ viṣṇur vismartavyo na jātu cit*  
*sarve vidhi-niṣedhāḥ syur etayor eva kiṅkarāḥ*

**Kṛṣṇa is the origin of Lord Viṣṇu. He should always be remembered and never forgotten at any time. All the rules and prohibitions mentioned in the** ***śāstras*** **should be the servants of these two principles.**

Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi counts adherence to rules and regulations for their own sake, without using them as a means to remember Kṛṣṇa, among the obstacles that spoil *bhakti*. In regard to our discussion: Mindless chanting just for the sake of sticking to our *sādhana* will prove fruitless or even counterproductive in the long run. Mindless chanting does not help us come closer to Kṛṣṇa.

This is an important point in our discussion about the proper mental attitude. Like with any other devotional activity, our chanting of *gāyatri* should be based firmly on a proper conception; we should know why and for whom we are chanting.

**Preparation: The** ***bhūta-śuddhi*** **prayers**

It is beneficial to prayerfully recite the following three wonderful verses before chanting the *gāyatri-mantras*. Although reciting these mantras first may not always be possible, it will certainly help us to chant them regularly.

These prayers remind us of our eternal spiritual identity and are therefore called the *bhūta-śuddhi* prayers.

*Bhūta* means “material elements” and *śuddhi* means “to be purified.” *Bhūta-śuddhi* is a process where the elements of the body are meditatively dismantled and then reconstituted in a purified, revitalized form. *Bhūta* can also refer to anything that exists, including any living being. Thus *bhūta-śuddhi* can also be translated, “purification of one’s existence.” In either case, for *vaiṣṇavas*, the practice and result of *bhūta-śuddhi* is to remember our true spiritual nature: When we are free from material influence, we enter our spiritual identity and are thus able to perform eternal constitutional service to Śri Kṛṣṇa.

*Pūjāris* about to enter the Deity room are advised to chant these prayers. Similarly, we can derive benefit from them when we worship the Deities presiding over the *gāyatri-mantras.* We should chant these prayers with a desire to purify the bodily concept of life because they can focus one on one’s identity as the servant of Lord Kṛṣṇa’s eternal servants. This practice quickly helps us to become aloof from material consciousness and establish the mind on the platform of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

***Verse 1***

**I am by nature the eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa, but because of my misfortune I have been inimical toward Him from time immemorial. Therefore I have identified myself with my body and wandered continuously in the cycle of birth and death in the material world, suffering the burning, threefold miseries. Now as a result of some unimaginable good fortune, my spiritual master’s mercy has enabled me to know that I am Kṛṣṇa’s eternal servant, an individual spiritual being, completely apart from the gross and subtle body. So by the order of my spiritual master I have obtained the good fortune of serving his lotus feet and, following in his footsteps, the lotus feet of Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Śri Śri Rādhā-Śyāmasundara.**

***Verse 2***

*nāhaṁ vipro na ca nara-patir nāpi vaiśyo na śūdro*  
*nāhaṁ varṇi na ca gṛha-patir no vanastho yatir vā*  
*kintu prodyan-nikhila-paramānanda-pūrṇāmṛtābdher*  
*gopi-bhartuḥ pada-kamalayor dāsa-dāsānudāsaḥ*

**I am not a** ***brāhmaṇa*****, I am not a** ***kṣatriya*****, and I am not a** ***vaiśya*****, or a** ***śūdra*****. Nor am I a** ***brahmacari*****, a householder, a** ***vānaprastha*****, or a** ***sannyāsi*****. I identify Myself only as the servant of the servant of the servant of the lotus feet of Lord Śri Kṛṣṇa, the maintainer of the** ***gopis*****. He is like an ocean of nectar, and He is the cause of universal transcendental bliss. He is always existing with brilliance.**

***Verse 3***

*divyaṁ Śri-hari-mandirādhya-tilakaṁ kaṇṭhaṁ sumālānvitaṁ*  
*vakṣaḥ śri-hari-nāma-varṇa-subhagaṁ śri-khaṇda-liptaṁ punaḥ*  
*pūtaṁ sūkṣmaṁ navāmbaraṁ vimalatāṁ nityaṁ vahantiṁ tanuṁ*  
*dhyāyet Śri guru-pāda-padma-nikaṭe sevotsukaṁ cātmanaḥ*

**One should meditate on oneself situated at the feet of one’s spiritual master, one’s pure body dressed in fine new cloth and decorated with wonderful** ***tilaka*****, one’s chest marked with the holy name and anointed with sandalwood pulp, and one’s neck bedecked with a garland.**

**The jewel of full attention**

In Harināma-cintāmaṇi Śrila Bhaktivinoda Thākura calls inattention the root cause of all offenses against the holy name. These offenses restrict the natural growth of pure devotion. One can chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra for thousands of years and still not receive the ultimate benefit of pure Kṛṣṇa consciousness as long as one remains inattentive.

The same principle applies to *gāyatri-japa.* One may chant the sacred *gāyatri-mantras* for a long time, but if he is inattentive in his practice, he will not receive the real benefit. Since the principles of maintaining proper attention are the same as when chanting the holy name, and since I have already elaborately presented them in the Nectarean Ocean of the Holy Name, I will address them here only briefly, simply to remind and encourage us to endeavor for full attention while chanting both *gāyatri* and the holy name.

Inattention is synonymous with negligence. Therefore the scriptures have referred to inattention by using the strong word, *pramāda*, “madness.” If a host invites an important guest and then neglects him, being engaged in some other activity, the host would be considered impolite or even offensive. Why did he invite the guest if he simply plans to ignore him? This example describes the state of our mind when we invite Kṛṣṇa into our heart by chanting *gāyatri-* or *nāma-japa,* and then engage in some other activity, ignoring our guest. To be inattentive while chanting is simply a form of madness.

Inattention can be divided into three categories:

1\. *Audāsinya*: indifference or lack of attraction or enthusiasm. We see this in those who perform their devotional service with apathy, as if caught up in a tasteless, daily routine. The best way to overcome *audāsinya* is to take shelter of *sādhu-saṅga*, and especially of those who are serious and dedicated chanters.

2\. *Jadya*: laziness or even inertia. The main symptom of laziness is a lack of enthusiasm. How to surcharge enthusiasm? As wanderers in the desert will find oases where they can replenish their water supplies, devotees can similarly renew their enthusiasm by visiting sources of enthusiasm. Associate with dedicated devotees of Kṛṣṇa, straighten your *sādhana*, and most importantly, hear of Lord Kṛṣṇa! *Kṛṣṇa-kathā* affects the soul like a pilgrimage to a holy place. To avoid or neglect such visits to the wells of enthusiasm is tantamount to spiritual suicide. To cultivate them regularly frees one from spiritual inertia and attracts the soul to Kṛṣṇa.

3\. *Vikṣepa*: distraction. This refers to paying attention to what is going on around one instead of focusing on the chanting. *Vikṣepa* is best cured by practicing in an environment free from distraction, such as before a sacred Tulasi plant, in association with dedicated devotees, or in seclusion.

We should become resolved to overcome these mental thieves who steal the fruits of our chanting of both the holy name and the *gāyatri-mantras*. When we are successful in this attempt, we will taste the sweet nectar of the jewel of full attention.

***Smaraṇa*** **– meditation**

A devotee who has been engaged in *śravaṇa* and *kirtana* naturally comes to *smaraṇa*, remembrance of and meditation on Kṛṣṇa. In our *gaudiya-vaiṣṇava* tradition, *smaraṇa* should always be practiced in conjunction with *nāma-saṅkirtana*, because only if the mind is sufficiently purified by chanting the holy name can the *sādhaka* meditate properly, free from the mental platform of acceptance and rejection according to mundane conceptions. Jiva Gosvāmi states that *smaraṇa* can be practiced in two ways:

1\. *Svārasiki*: spontaneous meditation in which one follows the natural flow of *rasas* that are manifesting one after another in the purified mind. (*Sva* – own; *ā-rasiki* – following rasa.) This is the platform of perfected *rāgānuga-bhakti*.

2\. *Mantropāsanā*: receiving help from a mantra that addresses Kṛṣṇa in a particular way (*mantra* – of a mantra; *upāsanā* – adoration, worship; literally: “sitting or being near”). While chanting such a mantra one meditates on Kṛṣṇa according to the specific meaning of that mantra.

Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi further offers a beautiful picture to distinguish these two kinds of practice:

*tatra nānā-lilā-pravāha-rūpatayā svārasiki gaṅgeva.*  
*ekaika-lilātmatayā mantropāsanā-mayi tu labdha-tat-*  
*sambhava-hrada-śreṇir iva jñeyā.*

**The practice of** ***svārasiki*** **can be compared with the flow of the Gaṅgā, as there are many different pastimes (*****nānā-lilā*****) that blend into one another continuously. The practice of** ***mantropāsanā*****, on the other hand, resembles a range of lakes (*****hrada-śreṇi*****) created by the Gaṅgā, which are each filled with one particular pastime (*****eka-eka-lilā-ātmatayā*****). Since one generally meditates upon such a** ***lilā*** **while chanting a mantra, this practice is called** ***mantropāsanā-mayi*****.**

In other words, in *mantropāsanā* one meditates on one specific aspect of Kṛṣṇa’s qualities and pastimes and nourishes his meditation by becoming mentally absorbed in the meaning of the specific mantra. The *gāyatri-mantras* are perfectly suitable for *mantropāsanā* meditation, as we will see in the next chapter, where we discuss their inner meanings.

The practice of *svārasiki* is quite advanced; the “restless” gopis of Goloka Vṛndāvana are the personification of its perfection (*svārasiki-siddhi*), as Śrila Bhaktivinoda Thākura states:

**The supremely chaste and restless** ***gopis*** **of Vraja are the treasury of** ***svārasiki-siddhi*****. The meditation of yogis and the knowledge of impersonalists cannot reach that stage. On such a platform, one can directly see the Lord during His midday pastimes. O servants of the lotus feet of Rādhā! Please engage me in appropriate service to Śri Rādhā according to the moment’s need.**

The process of *smaraṇa* is divided into five progressive stages according to the *sādhaka’s* level of remembrance. Going through these stages will bring one to the highest platform of directly realizing Kṛṣṇa:

1\. *Smaraṇa* is the beginning stage wherein one consciously tries to focus the mind on Śri Hari’s names, forms, and so on. Since the beginner’s mind is uncontrolled, agitated, and restless, meditation at this level is only partial, constantly interrupted by distracting thoughts. The ability to concentrate is limited.

2\. *Dhāraṇa*, absorption, develops when by repeated effort the mind begins to be purified. Then the *sādhaka* is able to at least occasionally withdraw the mind from external objects and fix it on the Lord, if only in a general way.

3\. *Dhyāna*, meditation, is the mature stage of absorption. Whereas in *dhāraṇa* the mind’s focus is general, in *dhyāna* one is able to specifically contemplate the Lord’s names, forms, etc.

4\. *Dhruva-anusmṛti*, constant remembrance, develops when the *sādhaka* learns to meditate (or chant) without offense (*dhruva* – constant, unwavering; *anu* – following in the footsteps of the previous *ācāryas*; *smṛti* – remembrance). Then meditation on Kṛṣṇa’s outstanding qualities invokes an uninterrupted and unconstrained flow of remembrance – a continuous flow of nectar that overwhelms the devotee’s heart with transcendental bliss.

5\. *Samādhi*, spiritual rapture, is the mature stage of *dhruva-anusmṛti*. Without separate effort the devotee is able to meditate deeply on Kṛṣṇa and be completely captured by Him. Śri Kṛṣṇa becomes the sole object of the devotee’s thinking, feeling, and desiring.

The perfection of Kṛṣṇa conscious *samādhi* is called *āpana*, complete assimilation. At this stage, the Lord’s internal energy sweeps away all remaining material concepts and identifications. No longer disturbed by either this world’s unpleasant or pleasant events, the devotee attains the transcendental platform of *bhāva* and becomes fixed in continuous meditation on Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana. Since *bhāva* is the blossoming stage of *prema*, *samādhi* will lead one to prema when one’s *bhāva* blossoms by developing its mature state. We can attain *samādhi* by chanting the *gāyatri-mantras*.

***Manana*** **– a life of reflection**

Chanting the *gāyatri-mantras* is a life-long activity that will ultimately help us to mature into higher states of realization. To reach those states requires a constant process of reflection or absorption called *manana*. This is the whole essence of spirituality. In the words of Socrates, “The unexamined life is not worth living.” Chanting *gāyatri* absentmindedly, without reflection, is a shallow experience. The *śāstras* encourage us:

*mananāt pāpatas trāti mananāt svargam aśnute*  
*mananād mokṣam āpnoti cātur-varga-mayo bhavet*

**Through reflection (*****manana*****) one is delivered from sinful reactions. Through reflection one enjoys heaven. And through reflection one obtains liberation and all the four goals of human life.**

There is a difference between *smaraṇa* and *manana*, although both refer to mental absorption. *Smaraṇa* is one of the nine processes of *sādhana-bhakti* described by Prahlāda Mahārāja. It is a practice, as part of one’s daily *sādhana*, to focus the mind on the Lord without allowing it to be diverted to any other subject. *Manana* is the conscious reflection and deliberation of what has been heard, by deep thought, intellectual consideration, and discussion. Being a life-long endeavor of becoming truly self-conscious and Kṛṣṇa conscious, *manana* leads to realization and assimilation of Kṛṣṇa consciousness in one’s life.

*Gāyatri* is meant to help us achieve this perfection. For this end one should practice the following six steps while chanting the *gāyatri- mantras*:

1\. One should remember the meaning of the mantras.

2\. One should search out the *mantra-devatas*, the deities of the mantras, in meditation. The deities for the different *gāyatri- mantras* are:  
a) *brahma-gāyatri* Kṛṣṇa or Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa;  
b) *guru-gāyatri* the spiritual master as *mukunda-preṣṭha*, he who is dear to Lord Mukunda and who gives Him pleasure;  
c) *gaura-gāyatri* Śri Gaurāṅga Mahāprabhu;  
d) *gopāla-gāyatri* Śri Gopāla, the Lord of Vṛndāvana;  
e) *kāma-gāyatri* the Divine Couple Śri Śri Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in Their divine pastimes.

3\. One should meditate on one’s relationship with these deities.

4\. One should take exclusive shelter at their lotus feet.

5\. One should practice the six limbs of *śaraṇāgati*, which will lead to the final step.

6\. One should practice the path of *ātma-nivedana*, full self-surrender without reservation.

One who finds these six steps too complicated may observe the following two rules:

1\. Chant with full attention.

2\. While chanting, meditate with affection on the meaning of the mantras.

**How emperor Akbar learned an important lesson**

The famous Mogul emperor of India, Akbar the Great, once took a religious retreat to read the Koran at a mosque. As he attempted to absorb himself in the holy scripture, he found himself repeatedly distracted by his restless mind. Thus he could not help but notice a young woman pacing to and fro in the street in front of the mosque, obviously troubled.

Suddenly she entered the mosque, crossed the hall, and without even seeing him, stumbled over Akbar. Without stopping she continued her mysterious search as the holy Koran fell to the floor with a thud. Akbar was shocked. Composing himself with difficulty he called out, “Guards! Guards! Bring me that young infidel woman!” The guards captured the woman and brought her before Akbar. “You are ruthless,” Akbar shouted. “I condemn you to be hanged. You have dared to run over your emperor and to desecrate the Koran, and you didn’t even notice!”

The young woman slowly returned to her senses. She then excused herself from the bottom of her heart. Akbar was intrigued. “Before you die, tell me what kept you so intensely absorbed, so much so that you did not even notice your offense against me and the holy book.”

The young woman replied, “This morning, Emperor, I heard that my lover had arrived in town. I was searching for him all over. I’m very sorry, but I really did not notice you.”

This only made Akbar more furious. “You have dared to offend the holy Koran and kick me with your feet for such an insignificant reason? You will be hanged by a thick rope so that your death will be slow. Guards, execute her!”

The young woman, realizing she had nothing to lose, requested the emperor if she could ask one last question. Akbar conceded. “Respected emperor,” she said, “may I ask what you were doing at the moment I ran over you?”

Akbar couldn’t believe his ears. “I already told you – I was reading the Koran!”

At this the young woman replied with intensity, “If you really had been reading the holy Koran, you would have been searching for the greatest lover. If I, who was searching for an ordinary lover of this world, was so absorbed that I did not notice you, how undisturbed should you have been if you had really been searching for God.” Now, she became stern. “You were not reading the Koran. You were not praying. You were not looking for God. You were only pretending to do these things. Now that I have said that, you are free to hang me.”

As these stories go, Akbar recognized her point and set her free.

But this story offers us an important lesson in our own attempt to remember the Lord. Śrila Prabhupāda writes in his introduction to the Bhagavad-gitā:

Therefore, we have to practice remembering the Lord always, twenty-four hours a day by chanting His names and molding our lives’ activities in such a way that we can remember Him always.

How is this possible? The *ācāryas* give the following example. If a married woman is attached to another man, or if a man has an attachment for a woman other than his wife, then the attachment is to be considered very strong. One with such an attachment is always thinking of the loved one. The wife who is thinking of her lover is always thinking of meeting him, even while she is carrying out her household chores. In fact, she carries out her household work even more carefully so her husband will not suspect her attachment. Similarly, we should always remember the supreme lover, Śri Kṛṣṇa, and at the same time perform our material duties very nicely. A strong sense of love is required here. If we have a strong sense of love for the Supreme Lord, then we can discharge our duty and at the same time remember Him. But we have to develop that sense of love.

In the above purport Śrila Prabhupāda refers to a beautiful verse, which appears in a letter written by Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu. When Rūpa and Sanātana Gosvāmis were still engaged as ministers at Nawab Hussain Shah’s court, they wrote Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu several letters begging him for spiritual advice. In His reply, Lord Caitanya highlighted how one can develop intense love for Kṛṣṇa by quoting this śloka:

*para-vyasanini nāri vyagrāpi gṛha-karmasu*  
*tad evāsvādayaty antar nava-saṅga-rasāyanam*

**If a woman is attached to a man other than her husband, she will appear very busy in carrying out her household affairs, but within her heart she is always relishing feelings of association with her par-amour.**

We should note that this advice was given to devotees engaged in so-called worldly affairs – although we should never forget that Rūpa and Sanātana Gosvāmi were constantly absorbed in love of God. Nevertheless, we can safely assume that the Lord’s words are relevant to all *vaiṣṇavas* still engaged in this world. While externally performing mundane duties, such devotees can meditate on the Lord and thus develop a sweet mood of attachment for Him. Why else would Śrila Prabhupāda quote this verse in such an important text as the introduction to the most essential of our scriptures? This instruction is meant to encourage us as well, not only Rūpa Gosvāmi and Sanātana Gosvāmi: We should always practice to remember Kṛṣṇa, regardless of our external circumstances.

The word “remember” suggests that meditating on Kṛṣṇa is an inherent faculty of the living entity. Love of Godhead is asleep in the heart of every conditioned soul, waiting to be awakened. The *gāyatri-mantras* are extremely glorious in helping the conditioned souls to become liberated from matter and direct their attention to the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

## 6. Three stages of contemplation

Let us look now at an important inner aspect of the *gāyatri-mantras,* namely, the three stages of contemplation, *sambandha*, *abhidheya*, and *prayojana*. These three stages of contemplation are not only relevant in *gāyatri-japa*; understanding them is a basic requirement for any person wishing to enter the deeper imports of the Vedas.

**The three subjects of vedic education**

Vedic spiritual education can be classified into three fundamental subjects:

***1. Sambandha***

*Sambandha-jñāna* (*sam-bandha* – connection, relationship, binding) refers to knowledge about *sambandha-tattva* – the relationship between the Supreme Lord (*iśvara*), the living entity (*jiva*), and the material energy (*prakṛti*). The *jiva’s* identification with *prakṛti* is only temporary and illusory. Both *jiva* and *prakṛti* are eternally and inseparably connected to the Supreme Lord, who is the true object of the *jiva’s* love. Each living entity is related to the Supreme Lord as servant, and He is their only master. This is our general *sambandha*. However, in the higher stages of *bhakti*, *sambandha* becomes more explicit as our specific relationship with the Lord, either as servant, friend, parent, or confidential lover. Finally, when we have factually attained the association of Śri Kṛṣṇa, *sambandha* becomes firmly and eternally established as *svarūpa-siddhi*, our unique personal identity in which we experience our particular relationship with the Lord in our eternal spiritual body.

**2.** ***Abhidheya***

*Abhidheya* refers to the means by which the ultimate goal is achieved, especially in relation to the practices of *sādhana-bhakti* (from the verbal root *abhi-dhā* – to set forth, explain, address). Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu explained this to Sanātana Gosvāmi:

*abhidheya-nāma “bhakti”, “prema” – prayojana*  
*puruṣārtha-śiromaṇi prema mahā-dhana*

**Devotional service, or sense activities for the satisfaction of the Lord \[*****sādhana*****\] is called abhidheya, because it can develop one’s original love of Godhead, which is the goal of life.**

The most important sources for our understanding of the various practices of *abhidheya* in the *gaudiya-vaiṣṇava* tradition have been compiled by Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi in Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu, and by Gopāla Bhaṭṭa Gosvāmi and Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi in Hari-bhakti-vilāsa.

**3.** ***Prayojana***

*Prayojana* refers to the living entities’ topmost interest and greatest wealth, *prema*, or pure love of Kṛṣṇa, along with engagement in His loving devotional service (from the verbal root *pra-yuj* – to unite with, turn \[the mind\] to, lead toward).

The “traditional” followers of the Vedas explain the human goal of life in terms of *dharma*, *artha*, *kāma*, and *mokṣa*. This is the *puruṣārtha*, the general path of human perfection. By following religious principles (*dharma*) one obtains material facilities such as wealth (*artha*), by which one is able to enjoy sense gratification (*kāma*). Finally, if by realizing the futility of these three processes one starts to strive for a higher goal, one obtains liberation from material existence (*mokṣa*).

The real essence of *prayojana*, however, lies far beyond the ambitions of the *dharmis*, *arthis*, *kāmis*, and *mokṣis*. It is directly aimed at the pure spiritual existence in loving union with Lord Kṛṣṇa.

*Sambandha*, *abhidheya*, and *prayojana* are the basic subjects of all Vedic education, as confirmed by Caitanya Mahāprabhu:

*veda-śāstra kahe – “sambandha”, “abhidheya”, “prayojana”*  
*“kṛṣṇa” – prāpya sambandha, “bhakti” – prāptyera sādhana*

**The Vedic literatures give information about the living entity’s eternal relationship with Kṛṣṇa, which is called** ***sambandha*****. The living entity’s understanding of this relationship and his acting accordingly is called** ***abhidheya*****. Returning home, back to Godhead, is the ultimate goal of life and is called** ***prayojana*****.**

Śrila Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Thākura defines these subjects in his own inimitable words:

**1. All efforts of our wisdom and consciousness to discover and attain our eternal relationship with the Supreme Reality, and consequently to know our properly adjusted or harmonized relationship with all animation, through that central relationship with the Truth is known as** ***sambandha-jñāna*****.**

**2. Following the regulative principles for the attainment of the highest goal of life – the purely blissful devotional service of the Supreme Lord Śri Kṛṣṇa-candra – is known as** ***abhidheya*****.**

**3. And tasting the supremely blissful nectarean fruits (*****prema-bhakti*****) of one’s genuine devotional practice is known as** ***prayojana*****.**

One can even say that *sambandha*, *abhidheya*, and *prayojana* are essential ingredients of proper education in any subject matter. Without knowing the connections and relationships, the methods, and the goal to be attained, how can one progress in any activity, what to speak of in the highest activity and the supreme goal of all, attaining the pure loving service of Bhagavān Śri Kṛṣṇa? As we will now see, these subjects are inseparably connected to the *gāyatri-mantras*.

***Sambandha*****,** ***abhidheya*****, and** ***prayojana*** **in the** ***gāyatri-mantras***

Since the subjects of *sambandha*, *abhidheya*, and *prayojana* are present in Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, they can also be found in the *gāyatri-mantras*, as Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmi confirms:

*gāyatrira arthe ei grantha-ārambhana*  
*“satyaṁ paraṁ” – sambandha, “dhimahi” – sādhana-prayojana*

**In the beginning of Śrimad-Bhāgavatam there is an explanation of the** ***brahma-gāyatri-mantra*****. “The Absolute Truth” (*****satyam param*****) indicates the relationship (*****sambhanda*****), and “we meditate on Him” (*****dhimahi*****) indicates the execution of devotional service (*****abhidheya*****) and the ultimate goal of life (*****prayojana*****).**

Indeed, the *brahma-gāyatri* and each of the other *gāyatri-mantras* is divided into three stages of contemplation (*sambandha*, *abhidheya*, and *prayojana*). Using the *kāma-gāyatri* as an example, Śrila Prabhupāda explains:

**In the** ***kāma-gāyatri*****, Kṛṣṇa is called Kāmadeva, Puṣpabāṇa and Anaṅga. Kāmadeva is Madana-mohana, the Deity who establishes our relationship with Kṛṣṇa \[*****sambandha-tattva*****\]. Puṣpabāṇa \[He who carries an arrow made of flowers\] is Govinda, the Personality of Godhead who accepts our service \[*****abhidheya-tattva*****\], and Anaṅga is Gopijanavallabha who satisfies all the** ***gopis*** **and is the ultimate goal of life \[*****prayojana-tattva*****\].**

In the *guru*-, *gaura*-, and *kāma-gāyatri*, the words *vidmahe*, *dhimahi*, and *pracodayāt* directly relate to these three stages:

1\. The verb *vidmahe* comes from the root *vid*, “to know, understand, find out.” This indicates the state of acknowledging one’s relationship with the Lord (*sambandha*).

2\. *Dhimahi* comes from the root *dhi*, “to think, reflect, or meditate.” Meditation refers to the process of worshiping the Lord that leads to *prema-bhakti* (*abhidheya*).

3\. *Pracodayāt* comes from the root *pra-cud*, “to set in motion, impel, or inspire.” *Pracodayāt* indicates *prayojana* because it refers to the attainment of *prema*, which impels the devotee to serve the Lord spontaneously.

Thus, as the following chart illustrates, we find these stages in all five *gāyatri-mantras*:

**The three stages of contemplation in the** ***gāyatri-mantras***

Sambandha  Abhidheya  Prayojana    Brahma-gāyatri  tat savitur…  bhargo devasya…  dhiyo yo…    Guru-gāyatri  gurudevāya…   kṛṣṇānandāya…  tan no guruḥ…    Gaura-gāyatri  caitanyāa…  viśvambharāya…   tan no gauraḥ…    Gopāla-gāyatri  kṛṣṇāya…  govindāya…  gopijana…    Kāma-gāyatri  kāmadevāya…   puṣpabāṇāya…   tan no ’naṅgaḥ…

This table helps us break each mantra into easily understandable chunks; each chunk offers us some kind of orientation. Of course, we should not lose sight of each mantra’s overall meaning. But to aid our meditation, we can, while chanting each mantra, divide it into these natural sections and pause for a small fraction of time between each of them. For example, while chanting the *kāma-gāyatri,* we can first meditate on *kāmadevāya*…, thereby becoming aware of the significance of praying to the Deity of *sambandha*. Then we can meditate on *puṣpabāṇāya*…, which allows us to concentrate on *abhidheya*. Finally we turn to *tan no ’naṅgaḥ*…, which helps us concentrate on *prayojana*.

## 7. A practical guideline

To conclude this chapter, we would like to offer our readers a five-step plan for effective chanting of their *gāyatri-japa*:

1\. Choose a good sitting place in a peaceful environment. If you are living somewhere, it is advisable to always choose the same seat for chanting *nāma*- and *mantra-japa*. Also, consider the points made before about proper time, place, and posture.

2\. Calm the mind and focus on what awaits you. By reciting these powerful mantras you will be worshiping your spiritual master, Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu, and Śri Śri Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa.

3\. Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi recommends making the mind peaceful by performing *prāṇāyāma* (breathing exercises). If you are not familiar with this intricate science, simply inhale and exhale deeply a few times.

4\. To fix the mind you might also like to perform *ācamana* (purification by sipping water) before you start your *japa*. Here is a simple guideline how to do *vaiṣṇava-ācamana*:

\- Find a proper sitting place (*āsana*). Then take an *ācamana-patra* (pot filled with water) and clean both hands by sprinkling them with water.

\- Holding the *ācamana* spoon in your left hand, pour a few drops into the right palm. While looking at the water, chant *oṁ keśavāya namaḥ*, and then silently sip the water from the base of the right thumb (the *brahma-tirtha*).

\- Repeat the same procedure while chanting *oṁ mādhavāya namaḥ.*

\- Repeat the same procedure while chanting *oṁ govindāya namaḥ*.

\- Afterwards, clean both hands with a few drops from the *ācamana-patra*.

\- Now chant the following mantra from the *ṛg-veda* while folding your palms together in front of your heart:

*oṁ tad-viṣṇoḥ paramaṁ padaṁ sadā paśyanti sūrayaḥ*  
*diviva cakṣur ātataṁ tad viprāso vipanyavaḥ*  
*jāgṛvāṁṣaḥ samindhate viṣṇor yat paramam padam*

**Just as those with ordinary vision see the sun rise in the sky, so the wise and learned devotees always see the supreme abode of Lord Viṣṇu (Kṛṣṇa). Because those highly praiseworthy and spiritually awake** ***brāhmaṇas*** **can see that abode they can also reveal it to others.**

5\. Chant the *bhūta-śuddhi* prayers and meditate on their meaning (see page 105).

As an alternative to following the numerous rules and regulations, it is said, one may simply follow a great soul’s example. For example, even if one does not perform the *puraścaraṇa* (ritualistic ceremonies for purification), he can become perfect in his chanting by serving his *guru* and following in his footsteps. This method is effective because by following a great soul, one comes to adopt his qualities. As bell metal becomes gold when treated with mercury, so a disciple takes on Viṣṇu’s qualities by associating with his *guru*.

## V. The bijas

Now we will enter the discussion of practical *gāyatri-japa*, mantra meditation, by looking at the specific mantras and their meanings. First we will study the different *bija-mantras* that precede the *gāyatri-mantras*, namely, *oṁ*, *kliṁ*, and *aiṁ*.

*Bija* means “seed.” As a tree’s seed contains a new tree in an unmanifest state, so each *bija-mantra* contains the meaning of the respective mantra condensed into one syllable.

Śrila Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura quotes the following verse to emphasize the importance of *bija-mantras*:

*vinā bijena mantrāṇāṁ viphalaṁ jāyate phalam*

**Without uttering a** ***bija*****, the desired fruits of mantras will not fructify.**

The Nārada-pañcarātra explains that the *bija-mantras* are primordial sound fragments that have the power to evoke a particular energy both within and outside of the person uttering them. In fact, each *bija-mantra* is qualitatively equal to a specific spiritual deity. The reason why one simple syllable has such amazing power lies in the subtle nature of Vedic sound. The letters of the Sanskrit alphabet are neither ordinary nor mundane. This has been confirmed by Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi in his work on Sanskrit grammar with the following *sūtra*:

*nārāyaṇād udbhūto ’yaṁ varṇa-kramaḥ*

**This order of Sanskrit letters has arisen from Nārāyaṇa.**

Each letter of the Sanskrit alphabet reflects a spiritual counterpart vibrating in its dormant state within a living entity bound by the material energy. In other words, each sound of the alphabet possesses a particular energetic quality. Some of these elementary sounds combine to form a new sound quality called a *bija*. When these *bijas* are chanted, their dormant sound potency is awakened.

There is another important aspect of chanting the *bija-mantras*: Since the *bijas* of the *gāyatri-mantras* represent the Deities invoked by these mantras, they have the strength to destroy the material impressions, attachments, and desires rooted deeply in our consciousness.

Each *bija-mantra* is related to one of Kṛṣṇa’s three principal energies, according to the Deity represented by the *bija*. These three energies and their respective Deities are:

1\. *Sat*, or *sandhini-śakti*: develops the variety of spiritual existence. It is related to Śri Kṛṣṇa.

2\. *Cit*, or *saṁvit-śakti*: manifests knowledge and relationship. It is related to Śri Balarāma.

3\. *Ānanda*, or *hlādini-sakti*: displays the bliss of spiritual emotions, relationships, and the exchange of *rasa*. It is related to Śrimati Rādhārāṇi.

From this we can see the eminent potency of the *bija-mantras.* In the following, we will therefore deal in detail with those *bijas* that precede the *gāyatri-mantras*.

## 1. Oṁ

*Oṁ* is the *sat-bija*. It invokes Lord Śri Kṛṣṇa’s divine presence. In Caitanya-caritāmṛta Śrila Prabhupāda gives a long and highly informative explanation of the sacred syllable *oṁ* (*oṁkāra*). Here are a few excerpts from this purport:

**Throughout all the Vedic literatures the glories of** ***oṁkāra*** **are specifically mentioned. Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi, in his thesis Bhāgavata-sandarbha, says that in the Vedic literature** ***oṁkāra*** **is considered to be the sound vibration of the holy name of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Only this vibration of transcendental sound can deliver a conditioned soul from the clutches of** ***māyā*****. Sometimes** ***oṁkāra*** **is also called the deliverer (*****tāra*****). Śrimad-Bhāgavatam begins with the** ***oṁkāra*** **vibration:** ***oṁ namo bhagavate vāsudevāya*****. Therefore** ***oṁkāra*** **has been described by the great commentator Śridhara Svāmi as** ***tāra-aṅkura*****, the seed of deliverance from the material world (*****tāra*** **– of deliverance;** ***aṅkura*** **– seed).**

Śrila Prabhupāda then elucidates on the benefits of chanting *oṁkāra*:

**In the Nārada-pañcarātra it is clearly said that the Supreme Personality of Godhead Nārāyaṇa personally appears before the chanter who engages in chanting the** ***aṣṭa-akṣara*****, or eight-syllable mantra,** ***oṁ namo nārāyaṇāya*****. A similar statement in the Maṇdūkya Upaniṣad declares that whatever one sees in the spiritual world is all an expansion of the spiritual potency of** ***oṁkāra*****.**

**On the basis of all the Upaniṣads, Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi says that** ***oṁkāra*** **is the Supreme Absolute Truth and is accepted as such by all the** ***ācāryas*** **and authorities.** ***Oṁkāra*** **is beginningless, changeless, supreme and free from deterioration and external contamination.** ***Oṁkāra*** **is the origin, middle, and end of everything, and any living entity who thus understands** ***oṁkāra*** **attains the perfection of spiritual identity in** ***oṁkāra*****.** ***Oṁkāra*****, being situated in everyone’s heart, is** ***iśvara*****, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, as confirmed in the Bhagavad-gitā (18.61):** ***iśvaraḥ sarva-bhūtānāṁ hṛd-deśe ’rjuna tiṣṭhati*****.** ***Oṁkāra*** **is as good as Viṣṇu because** ***oṁkāra*** **is as all-pervasive as Viṣṇu. One who knows** ***oṁkāra*** **and Lord Viṣṇu to be identical no longer has to lament or hanker. One who chants** ***oṁkāra*** **no longer remains a** ***śūdra*** **but immediately comes to the position of a** ***brāhmaṇa*****. Simply by chanting** ***oṁkāra*** **one can understand the whole creation to be one unit, or an expansion of the energy of the Supreme Lord. \[…\]**

**Since** ***oṁkāra*** **is the basic principle of all Vedic knowledge, it is uttered before one begins to chant any Vedic hymn. Without** ***oṁkāra*****, no Vedic mantra is successful.**

Then Śrila Prabhupāda describes the more confidential meaning of *oṁkāra* as it is especially revealed in our Gaudiya-Vaiṣṇava tradition:

***Oṁkāra*** **is a combination of the letters** ***a*****,** ***u*** **and** ***m*****.** ***A-kāreṇa ucyate kṛṣṇaḥ*****: the letter** ***a*** **(*****a-kāra*****) refers to Kṛṣṇa, who is** ***sarva-loka-eka-nāyakaḥ*****, the master of all living entities and planets, material and spiritual.** ***Nāyaka*** **means “leader.” He is the supreme leader (*****nityo nityānāṁ cetanaś cetanānām*****). The letter** ***u*** **(*****u-kāra*****) indicates Śrimati Rādhārāṇi, the pleasure potency of Kṛṣṇa, and** ***m*** **(*****ma-kāra*****) indicates the living entities (*****jivas*****). Thus** ***oṁ*** **is the complete combination of Kṛṣṇa, His potency and His eternal servitors.**

Śrila Prabhupāda refers here to the following verse from Śrila Rūpa Gosvāmi:

*a-kāreṇocyate kṛṣṇaḥ sarva-lokaika-nāyakaḥ*  
*u-kāreṇocyate rādhā ma-kāro jiva-vācakaḥ*

**By the letter** ***a*** **\[in** ***oṁ*****\], Kṛṣṇa is addressed, the Lord of all the worlds. By the letter** ***u*****, Rādhā is addressed, and the letter** ***ma*** **refers to the** ***jivas*****.**

In other words, *oṁ* is not at all impersonal. Nowadays, *oṁ* is often considered nothing more than a representation of the impersonal Brahman. Many devotees of Kṛṣṇa, working under this misconception, have even developed an aversion to chanting *oṁ*. One should understand, however, that *oṁ* clearly denotes Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa confirms this important point three times in the Bhagavad-gitā:

*vedyaṁ pavitraṁ oṁkāraḥ*

**I am the object of knowledge, the purifier and the syllable** ***oṁ*****.**

*praṇavaḥ sarva-vedeṣu*

**I am the syllable** ***oṁ*** **in the Vedic mantras.**

*oṁ ity ekākṣaraṁ brahma vyāharan mām anusmaran*  
*yaḥ prayāti tyajan dehaṁ sa yāti paramāṁ gatim*

**After being situated in this** ***yoga*** **practice and vibrating the sacred syllable** ***oṁ*****, the supreme combination of letters, if one thinks of the Supreme Personality of Godhead and quits his body, he will certainly reach the spiritual planets.**

For further enlightenment we recommend one study Śrila Prabhupāda’s entire purport.

## 2. Aiṁ

The mantra *aiṁ* is called the *cit-bija*. It is meant to invoke divine knowledge, and it represents Lord Balarāma. *Aiṁ* appears in the *guru-gāyatri* because the spiritual master is Śri Balarāma’s representative, as Śrila Prabhupāda explains:

**Baladeva acts as the spiritual master of all devotees, and by His causeless mercy the fallen souls are delivered. \[…\] Therefore it is particularly mentioned herein that Balarāma is the protector of the devotees of the Lord. By His divine grace only one can approach the Supreme Lord Śri Kṛṣṇa, and thus Śri Balarāma is the mercy incarnation of the Lord, manifested as the spiritual master, the savior of the pure devotees.**

*Aiṁ* is also called *vāg-bhāva-bija*. Referring to the “true nature of speech” (*vāg* – speech; *bhāva* – true nature, state of being, condition), it belongs to Sarasvati Devi, the goddess of learning and speech.

## 3. Kliṁ

*Kliṁ* is called the *ānanda-bija.* It invokes the divine presence of Śri Śri Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in Their mutual *lilā*. Since Caitanya Mahāprabhu is a combined form of the Divine Couple, *kliṁ* is also used in His mantra.

*Kliṁ* is also known as the *kāma-bija.* It is the seed of the fulfillment of all *kāma*, or desire. On one level, as we will see below, this refers to all material desires from which this material world in its vast variety has come into being. Ultimately, however, *kliṁ* represents the spiritual desire of Bhagavān Śri Kṛṣṇa to taste *rasa* (*raso vai saḥ*).

There is a significant difference between material and spiritual desire. Material life is based on the aspiration to enjoy sense objects. Such desire arises from the feeling of incompleteness resulting from false ego – from the soul’s misidentification with matter. Spiritual desire, on the other hand, arises out of completeness. It is ever joyful. Its material reflection is forced upon the bewildered soul by the senses and restless mind, and therefore it is not joyful. Material desire leaves the living entity with a sense of incompleteness, a constant sense of need. In a nutshell, the difference between material and spiritual desire is the difference between lust and pure love.

This is the inconceivable value of chanting *kliṁ*: By uttering the *gopāla-mantra* prefaced by the *kāma-bija*, we can move from the miserable world of material desire to the joyful life of *rasa*, to the life of loving Kṛṣṇa.

We should understand that this sacred syllable is not composed of ordinary letters; rather, *kliṁ* is the embodiment of Kṛṣṇa’s divine form. It is the Supreme Personality of Godhead’s inherent nature. Śrila Prabhupāda explains:

**The word** ***klim*** **added to the** ***gāyatri-mantra*** **is explained in Brahma-saṁhitā as the transcendental seed of love of Godhead, or the seed of the** ***kāma-gāyatri.*** **The object is Kṛṣṇa, who is the ever green Cupid, and by utterance of** ***klim-mantra*** **Kṛṣṇa is worshiped.**

Thus one way to enter into the mystery of the *kāma-bija* is to meditate on its identity with the divine form of Śri Kṛṣṇa. Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura reveals this confidential meditation by quoting the following verses. The first verse lists the five elements of the *kāma-bija*:

*pañcālaṅkāra-saṁyuktaṁ bijantu paramādbhutam*  
*ka-kāraś ca la-kāraś ca i-kāraś cārdha-candrakaḥ*  
*candra-binduś ca tad-yuktaṁ kāma-bijam udāhṛtam*

**The** ***kāma-bija kliṁ*** **(क्लीं) is supremely wonderful, being adorned with the following five ornaments:**

**1.   क    the letter** ***ka***

**2.   ल the letter** ***la***

**3. ि the letter** ***i***

**4. ॅ the half-moon symbol (*****ardha-candra*** **or** ***nāda*****)**

**5. ं the moon-dot above that (*****candra-bindu*****)**

**That which is endowed with these five items is called** ***kāma-bija*****.**

Then we hear a description of how the different limbs of Kṛṣṇa’s transcendental body are found in *kliṁ*:

*atha Śri-kāma-bijasya śariraṁ vigrahātmakam*  
*śri-kṛṣṇa-śarirābhinnāny- akṣarāṇi kramāt śrṇu*  
*ka-kāreṇa śiro bhālo bhrūr-nāsā-netra-karṇakau*  
*la-kāreṇa bhaved gaṇdas tad-danto hanu-rūpakaḥ*  
*cibuko ’tha griva caiva kaṇṭhaḥ prṣṭhaś ca suvrata*  
*i-kāraḥ skandho bāhuś ca kaphoṇi-raṅguli-nakhaḥ*  
*ardha-candro vakṣas tuṇdaḥ pārśvo nābhiḥ kaṭis tathā*  
*candra-bindav uru-janu- jaṅgha gulphaś ca padakaḥ*  
*pārṣṇiś cāpy aṅguli caiva nakhendur api nāradaḥ*  
*iti vigraha-rūpaś ca kāma-bijātmako hariḥ*

**O Nārada Muni, listen to the description of the bodily form of the** ***kāma-bija*****. The letters of this** ***bija*** **are nondifferent from the transcendental body of Śri Kṛṣṇa.**

**1. The क (*****ka*****) is seen as His head, forehead, eyebrows, nose, eyes, and ears.**

**2. The ल (*****la*****) represents His cheeks, teeth, jaw, chin, neck, throat, and back.**

**3. The  ि (*****i*****) is identical with His shoulders, arms, elbows, fingers, and fingernails.**

**4. The  ॅ (*****ardha-candra*****) is His chest, belly, two flanks, navel, and waist.**

**5. The ं (*****bindu*****) represents His thighs, knees, calves, ankles, feet, heels, toes, and His moonlike toenails.**

Later in the discussion of *kāma-gāyatri* we will hear of Kṛṣṇa’s five flower arrows, by which He completely captures the devotee’s senses and mind. According to Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura, each of the five elements of *kliṁ* is also assigned to one of Kṛṣṇa’s five flower arrows:

*bijākṣaraṁ pañca-puṣpa- bāṇa-tulyaṁ kramāt śrṇu*  
*ka-kāraś cāmra-mukulo la-kāraś cāśokaḥ smṛtaḥ*  
*i-kāro mallikā-puṣpaṁ mādhavi cārdha-candrakaḥ*  
*binduś ca bakula-puṣpam ete bāṇāḥ syur eva ca*

**Listen as I describe how the letters of this** ***bija*** **are equal to the five flower arrows (*****pañca-puṣpa-bāṇa*****) of Lord Kṛṣṇa:**

**1. The क (*****ka*****) is a mango bud (*****āmra-mukula*****).**

**2. The ल (*****la*****) is the magnificent red** ***aśoka*** **flower.**

**3. The  ि (*****i*****) is a jasmine (*****mallikā*****).**

**4. The  ॅ (*****ardha-candra*****) is the “spring flower”** ***mādhavi*****.**

**5. The ं (*****bindu*****) is a flower from the evergreen** ***bakula*** **tree.**

**In this way the five letters of the** ***kāma-bija*** **are the five kinds of flower arrows.**

Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura continues by revealing how the *kāma-bija* is identical with Śri Śri Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa and Their confidential pastimes:

*ka-kāraḥ puruṣah kṛṣṇaḥ sac-cid-ānanda-vigraḥ*  
*i-kāraḥ prakṛti rādhā nitya-vṛndāvaneśvari*  
*laś cānandātmakaṁ prema- sukhaṁ tayoś ca kirtitam*  
*cumban-ānanda-mādhuryaṁ nāda-binduḥ sam-iritaḥ*

**1. The क (*****ka*****) is Kṛṣṇa, the supreme** ***puruṣaḥ*****, who is the embodiment of** ***sac-cid-ānanda*****.**

**2. The  ि (*****i*****) is Rādhā, the** ***prakṛti*** **or** ***hlādini-śakti*** **of Kṛṣṇa, who is the eternal queen of Śri Vṛndāvana-dhāma.**

**3. The ल (*****la*****) is celebrated as the pure blissful joy of Their mutual ecstatic love (*****prema-sukha*****), which is the very nature of Their limitless happiness (*****ānanda-ātmaka*****).**

**4. The** ***ँ*** **(*****nāda and bindu conjoined*****) are described as** ***cumban-ānanda-mādhuryam*****, or the sweetness of Their blissful kissing (*****cumban*****).**

The *kāma-bija* also has an important material function. Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura describes how Lord Brahmā used the syllable *kliṁ*, which consists of five items, to create the five material elements:

*kliṁ-kārāda-sṛjad viśvam iti prāha śruteḥ śiraḥ*  
*la-kārāt pṛthivi jātā ka-kārāt jala-sambhavaḥ*  
*i-kārāt vahnir utpanno nādād vāyur ajāyata*  
*bindor ākaśa-sambhūtir iti bhūtātmako manuḥ*

**As the main** ***śrutis*** **state, Lord Brahmā created the material universe from the syllable** ***kliṁ*****.**

**1. From the letter** ***la*****, the element earth came into being.**

**2. The element water was created by the letter** ***ka*****.**

**3. From the letter** ***i*** **the fire element was born.**

**4. Air came from the** ***nāda*** **\[the** ***ardha-candra*****\].**

**5. Ether was created from the** ***bindu*** **\[the moon-dot,** ***candra-bindu*****\].**

**In this way, the material creation is composed of five elements.**

From the above description we can see that *kāma-bija* is the root cause of the entire material creation. This is, of course, only a side effect of this mantra’s great spiritual power. We should always be aware that with our material mind we can in no way even grasp the unlimited vastness of Vedic sound. Like *oṁ*, *kliṁ* is not just a sound, it is an ocean of sound. The deeper we dive into its understanding, the more secrets manifest their hidden meanings.

The various ways of meditating on *kāma-bija* described in this section are summarized in the following chart:

**Meditation on** ***Kāma-bija***

Letter of Kãma-bïja Element  
Created Flower

## VI. The mantras

Now we will present the five *gāyatri-mantras* and the two *mūla-mantras* in the same sequence they are chanted, beginning with *brahma-gāyatri* and ending with *kāma-gāyatri.*

There is a deep meaning to this order. Just as a mango is green in the beginning but develops its full taste and fragrance only when it is ripe, the *brahma-gāyatri* is considered the beginning mantra that later fully matures into the *kāma-gāyatri.* On the way to full ripeness, three additional meditations are required, namely, meditation on *guru* (*guru-mūla* and *guru-gāyatri*), Caitanya Mahāprabhu (*gaura-mūla* and *gaura-gāyatri*), and Śri Kṛṣṇa (*gopāla-gāyatri*). Only by these meditations will the *sādhaka* gain entrance into the Supreme Lord’s loving pastimes.

Each of the *mūla*- and *gāyatri-mantras* will be presented in four steps:

1\. an explanation of each word

2\. the translation of the whole mantra, as given by Śrila Prabhupāda

3\. further illumination on certain words and concepts

4\. an inspiring meditation

**Why hide the mantras?**

The *gāyatri-mantras* must be handed down from *guru* to disciple; they should be kept concealed. It is a general rule in spiritual life that confidential subjects leave the heart of the *sādhaka* if they are publicly disclosed. To underline this principle, Śrila Sanātana Gosvāmi has quoted the following verse from Sammohana-tantra:

*gopayed devatām iṣṭāṁ gopayed gurum ātmanaḥ*  
*gopayec ca nijaṁ mantraṁ gopayen nija-mālikām*

**One should hide one’s** ***iṣṭa-deva*****, one should hide one’s** ***guru*****, one should hide one’s mantra, and one should hide one’s** ***japa-mālā*****.**

Not only should we keep our mantras and the *iṣṭa-deva* confidential, but we should not disclose what services we render or donations we give, and should not discuss our particular relationship with *guru* and Kṛṣṇa. Instead of being *bahir-mukha,* “boasting outwardly,” Vaiṣṇavas should aspire to become *antar-mukha,* “facing inward.” Even Lord Kṛṣṇa keeps secrets, and His devotees should share that attitude with Him, as can be seen from this beautiful *śloka*:

*guhyāti guhya-goptā tvāṁ gṛhāṇāsmat-kṛtaṁ japam*  
*siddhir bhavatu me deva tvat-prasādāt tvayi sthite*

**O my Lord, you are the secret of secrets and a keeper of secrets. Please accept the** ***japa*** **I have chanted \[*****gāyatri-japa*** **or** ***nāma-japa*****\] as an offering to You. Please be merciful and let me attain the same perfection attained by those who are fixed in Your service.**

Śri Vāmana Deva expresses this principle as a general rule:

*sarvaṁ sampadyate devi deva-guhyaṁ susaṁvṛtam*

**That which is very confidential is successful if kept secert.**

One exception to this is the *mahā-mantra*. It is both confidential and meant for public distribution. Therefore we are free to share it with everyone. However, even the list of offenses against the holy name contains a point about not explaining the confidential glories of the holy name to the faithless.

The *dikṣā-mantras* (the *gāyatri-mantras*) belong to a different category. They should be kept confidential. For example, we do not display in public intimate letters or pictures reflecting a confidential relationship. Lovers are careful to keep their letters private, since the public might ridicule their contents. Similarly, the mantras are potent and could be misused. Please understand that these mantras have so much power that they can take us back to the spiritual world, but one could also misuse that power, especially the power of the *gopāla-gāyatri,* for material purposes (winning battles, etc.).

There may be exceptions to this general rule. For example, Śrila Prabhupāda quotes the complete *brahma*- and *kāma-gāyatris* in purports in the Caitanya-caritāmṛta. Nevertheless, in this book we will not quote the *guru-datta-mantras* in their original sequence. Candidates for these mantras will receive them from their spiritual master during *mantra-dikṣā.*

In his commentary on Bṛhad-Bhāgavatāmṛta, Gopiparāṇadhana Prabhu explains how far the scriptures sometimes go in concealing the *gāyatri-mantras*:

Vedic and tantric mantras should be chanted only by qualified persons who have received them through proper initiation. Therefore, when books discuss these mantras, special efforts are made to disguise the exact syllables of the mantra. Sometimes a few syllables are changed or inverted in the description, or sometimes the explanation is impossible to understand by those who are unfamiliar with the cryptic code being used. Thus Krama-dipikā, for example, describes the ten-syllable *gopāla-mantra* as follows:

*śārṅgi sottara-dantaḥ śūro vāmākṣi-yug-dvitiyo ’rnaḥ,*  
*śūli śaurir bālo bālānuja-dvayam athākṣara-catuṣkam*

**“The holder of the bow** ***śarṅga*****, the upper teeth, then śūra, and the second letter conjoined with the goddess Vāmākṣi, then the bearer of the trident, and** ***śauri*****, and Bala, and twice the younger brother of Bala, and four more syllables.”**

Of course, we will not go to such lengths in this book. Still, out of respect for the mantras, we will not present them exactly as they are spoken. Instead of explaining the words in their usual word-by-word order, we will write them down in their logical order (*anvaya*). This is a well-known method of presenting word-by-word translations of Sanskrit texts: the words are listed in the order that corresponds to the sentence structure of their English translation. Since in *anvaya* the word order differs from the original sequence, the mantras in their exact composition remain concealed to a certain degree.

**The Mūla-mantras**

Before both the *guru-gāyatri* and *gaura-gāyatri* we chant invocation mantras called *mūla-mantras*. *Mūla* means “root.” *Mūla-mantras* are the essence of the person they invoke; in fact, as sound representation of that person, they are nondifferent from him or her. They are used to address the Lord during Deity worship and are recited along with the offering of each article.

While chanting *gāyatri*, the *mūla-mantras* are chanted before the *gāyatri-mantras* to help fix our meditation on the person addressed in the respective *gāyatri-mantras* – either Śri Guru or Śri Gaurāṅga – by becoming aware of their name, form, etc.

## 1. The Brahma-gāyatri-mantra

**Explanation**

*oṁ* – (addressing the Supreme); *bhūḥ, bhuvaḥ, svaḥ* – the three planetary systems; *dhimahi* – let us meditate; *tat* – on that; *vareṇyam* – worshipable; *bhargaḥ* – effulgence; *savituḥ* – of the sun (Savitā); *devasya* – of the divine; *yaḥ* – who; *pracodayāt* – enthuses; *naḥ* – our; *dhiyaḥ* – meditation.

**Translation**

Let us meditate on that worshipable effulgence of the divine sun, who enthuses our meditation.

**Illumination**

Playing a fundamental role in all Vedic spiritual traditions, the *brahma-gāyatri-mantra* is also very important for the *vaiṣṇavas*.The Hari-bhakti-vilāsa states:

**The** ***brahma-gāyatri*** **is nondifferent from the Lord’s energy; it is the sound embodiment of His potency or śakti. The mantra is a meditation on the Supreme Lord in his functional aspect as the Lord of all energies, and as a creator, maintainer and destroyer of the universe.**

**Who is Savitā?**

Following the six-step method introduced in the section about *manana* (page 111), we will now seek the *mantra-devata*. Who is that sun-god, Savitā?

After having been initiated in the *brahma-gāyatri-mantra*, Śrila Prabhupāda’s disciples naturally asked the same question. Since we worship Śri Kṛṣṇa as the Supreme Lord, why would we address the sun-god in the *brahma-gāyatri*? Who is actually being referred to by *savitur*? One disciple wrote Śrila Prabhupāda for clarification and received the following illuminating answer:

**The meaning of the first mantra is “I meditate on the sun-god who is maintaining the three worlds.”** ***Savitur*** **is the name of the sungod, but the greatest** ***savitur*** **is Kṛṣṇa. Therefore, it is meditation on Kṛṣṇa who is the master of all the cosmic manifestation.**

According to the rules of Sanskrit grammar, the word *savitā* \[of which savitur is the genitive case\] can refer to any of the following:

\- the brahman effulgence

\- the sun

\- fire

\- our conscious nature as *jiva* souls

\- intelligence

\- Sarasvati Devi

\- Agni Deva

\- Sūrya Deva

\- Sūrya-Nārāyaṇa

\- Paramātmā

Herein lies the reason why the various *sampradāyas* and Vedic schools explain the *brahma-gāyatri* differently, as meditation on the sun-god, on impersonal Brahman, on Paramātmā, Viṣṇu, or Kṛṣṇa. The Gaudiya-Vaiṣṇava understanding aims at Śri Kṛṣṇa, the origin of all spiritual and material creation, who is behind the sun-god in His form as Sūrya-Nārāyaṇa. Śrila Jiva Gosvāmi explains:

**When the sun is mentioned as the object of worship in the** ***gāyatri*****, the sun should not be viewed as an independent entity but as indicating the Paramātmā. Śaunaka Ṛṣi confirms this in the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (12.11.28),** ***vyūhaṁ sūryātmano hareḥ*****, the Supreme Personality of Godhead Hari resides there as the presiding Deity of the sun.**

In one purport Śrila Prabhupāda clarifies the exalted position of the sun-god:

**In this regard, Śrila Viśvanātha Cakravarti Thākura says,** ***sūrya ātma ātmatvena upāsyaḥ*****. The actual life and soul of all living entities within this universe is the sun. He is therefore** ***upāsya*****, worshipable. We worship the sun-god by chanting the** ***\[brahma-\] gāyatri-mantra*****. Sūrya is the life and soul of this universe, and there are innumerable universes for which a sun-god is the life and soul, just as the Supreme Personality of Godhead is the life and soul of the entire creation.**

**We have information that Vairāja, \[a form of the Lord also called\] Hiraṇyagarbha entered the great, dull, material globe called the sun. \[…\] Bhagavad-gitā also says that Kṛṣṇa first instructed Bhagavad-gitā to the sun-god:** ***imaṁ vivasvate yogaṁ proktavan aham avyayam*****.**

In another purport, Śrila Prabhupāda identifies Savitā with Caitanya Mahāprabhu:

**Here Śri Rāmānanda Rāya admits that Śri Caitanya Mahāprabhu is the Supersoul. It is the Supersoul that inspires the devotee; therefore He is the original source of the** ***\[brahma-\] gāyatri-mantra*****. Savitā is the original source of all intelligence. That Savitā is Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu. This is confirmed in Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (2.4.22):**

*pracoditā yena purā sarasvati*  
*vitanvatājasya satiṁ smṛtiṁ hṛdi*  
*sva-lakṣaṇā prādurabhūt kilāsyataḥ*  
*sa me ṛṣiṇām ṛṣabhaḥ prasidatām*

**“May the Lord, who in the beginning of the creation amplified the potent knowledge of Brahmā from within his heart and inspired him with full knowledge of creation and His own self, and who appeared to be generated from the mouth of Brahmā, be pleased with me.” This was spoken by Śukadeva Gosvāmi when he invoked the blessing of the Supreme Personality of Godhead before delivering Śrimad-Bhāgavatam to Mahārāja Parikṣit.**

**The Vyāhṛtis**

The words *bhūḥ bhuvaḥ svaḥ* are sound representations of the three planetary systems, or those parts of the universe that are invigorated by the divine sun of Kṛṣṇa. *Bhūḥ*, earth, refers to Bhū-loka, the lower planetary system; *bhuvaḥ*, air, refers to Bhuvar-loka, the upper planetary system; and *svaḥ*, sky, refers to Svar-loka, the higher heavenly planets.

Above this plane are four even higher regions named Mahar-loka, Jano-loka, Tapo-loka, and Satya-loka. This makes a total of seven planetary systems. They are represented by the seven *vyāhṛtis* or utterances representing the seven worlds: *bhūḥ*, *bhuvaḥ*, *svaḥ*, *mahaḥ*, *janaḥ*, *tapaḥ*, and *satya*.

In the *brahma-gāyatri*, the four higher regions – *mahaḥ*, *janaḥ*, *tapaḥ*, and *satya* – are included in the word *svaḥ*. Therefore, the three words *bhūḥ bhuvaḥ svaḥ* are called the *mahā-vyāhṛtis*, “the great sacred utterances of the seven worlds.” They represent the universe in its totality. According to Śrila Prabhupāda, *bhūḥ* refers to Pātāla-loka, *bhuvaḥ* to Martya-loka, and *svaḥ* to Svarga-loka.

Śridhara Mahārāja (Śrila Prabhupāda’s godbrother) offers an interesting approach to the *mahā-vyāhṛtis*, by relating them to the three planes of material existence, namely the senses (*pṛthvi*), the mind (*antarikṣa*), and the intelligence (*svarga*). He suggests the following understanding:

1\. The first plane, *bhūḥ*, is the world of our experience, Bhū-loka, the place where we are physically present. This is the plane where the ten senses interact with their sense objects.

2\. The next plane, *bhuvaḥ*, is the world of mental activity. It is the subtle world of previous *saṁskāras*, impressions acquired in previous births according to our *karma*. These *saṁskāras* are embedded deeply in the mind. That we are here in our present body in the world of physical experience is not accidental; we have acquired this position by our previous *karma*. The physical world of experience is only the product, the outcome of our previous *saṁskāras*. Thus our present position in the world of sensual experience (Bhū-loka) is the effect of our experiences in the mental sphere, which is known as Bhuvar-loka.

3\. Above Bhuvar-loka is Svar-loka, the world of intelligence. The difference is that the mental world, Bhuvar-loka, is ruled by acceptance and rejection, because the mind accepts that which is sensually enjoyable and rejects everything else. The level of Svar-loka, however, is the plane of decision, the world of intelligence and reason (*buddhi-loka*). The intelligence accepts things that are favorable, even when they are not enjoyable, and vice versa.

In this way, *bhūḥ, bhuvaḥ*, and *svaḥ* reflect our “own universe,” our microcosm of the senses, mind, and intelligence. For some *yogis* – those who engage in the science of the subtle body – this is not a concept but a factual reality. They identify the seven main physical *cakras* with the seven planetary systems and also with the parts of *brahma-gāyatri*, as illustrated in the following chart:

**The Bodily Cakras, the Seven Lokas, and Brahma-gāyatri**

Level  Cakra   Location   Loka  Part of Brahma-gāyatri    7  sahasrāra  top of head  satya  dhiyo yo…    6  ājña  forehead  tapaḥ  bhargo devasya…    5  viśuddha  throat  janaḥ  vareṇyam    4  anāhata  heart   mahar  tat-savitur    3  maṇipura  solar plexus  svaḥ  svaḥ    2  svādhiṣṭhāna  lower abdomen  bhuvaḥ   bhuvaḥ    1  mūladhara  pelvis  bhūḥ    bhūr
